      minhash: PQwe6u+S09xaFf9s2UeEIzf3DtKhcwsKW5+rsAy5wWeE0qW2MkznP+SSbRSgSm2QiSQAEjrMFefxbqgJT5neHWolt6MquCGRwNdMlwOkw0ed140/CDwKaVKUWzdSqVJgqeWbz+n1Qnm/iV/C2o/o/6KaB1lAxwECH1zq78Wj1Rb8thVZSxHRa4bXKpsWClfav9p9bOI/W8a3M3ubEuiQaPJfhDAXkXCCtiPYlM+AFXun7CVQKDopIhdaYGqwJRjymQYSlrZrObT7j4ze1O1DMOgyCUk/yiXbAkKawsyCeblGW64Qc9qJaPTpTE6W4rckDM4t94Yqo2dSBXfiSEgWFQ==

---
<!-- assumption-1: body_md -->
\label{ass:basic}
    The coefficients are $\mathcal B_0$-measurable, and there exists a deterministic $C_0<\infty$, independent of $d$, such that, almost surely,
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:basic-coefficient-assumption}
        n\leq C_0p,
        \qquad
        \max_{0\,\leq\, t\,<\,T}\big(|b_t|+\gamma_t+d|c_t|+\gamma_t^{-1}\big)\,\leq\, C_0.
    \end{equation}
    Moreover, $\sigma_t\in\mathscr{C}_b^4(\mathbb R)$ for every $0\leq t<T$, with the functions and their derivatives through order four bounded uniformly in $d$.
<!-- end assumption-1 -->
<!-- assumption-2: body_md -->
\label{ass:signal-init}
    The pair $(S^\star,\widehat S^0)$ is independent of the two sensing ensembles and the observation noise. There exists $C_1<\infty$, independent of $d$, such that
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:signal-init-bound}
        d^{-1/2}\|S^\star\|_\mathrm{F}+\|\widehat S^0\|_\mathrm{op}\leq C_1
        \qquad\text{almost surely}.
    \end{equation}
<!-- end assumption-2 -->
<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{thm:final-universality}
    Let Assumptions \ref{ass:basic} and \ref{ass:signal-init} hold, and let $0\leq s,t\leq T$, $\mu\in\mathscr{C}^3(\mathbb R)$, and an integer $m\geq2$ be fixed. There exists $C_{m,\mu}<\infty$, independent of $d$, such that, for each of the three observables in \eqref{eq:observable-notation},
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:main-universality-moments}
        \left\lVert \Phi_\mathrm{RO}-\Phi_{\mathrm G}\right\rVert_{L^m}
        \leq C_{m,\mu}d^{-1/2}.
    \end{equation}
    Consequently,
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:main-universality-stochastic}
        \Phi_\mathrm{RO}-\Phi_{\mathrm G}=\mathcal{O}_\prec(d^{-1/2}).
    \end{equation}
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
The common coupling makes \eqref{eq:main-universality-moments} a direct comparison of the random observables. It is not essential: the proof separately compares their conditional means given $(S^\star,\widehat S^0)$ and controls their fluctuations, so the same rate follows for independent noise realizations provided the signal and initialization remain shared. The proof is assembled in Section~\ref{sec:proof-main}, using the comparison and concentration estimates developed in the subsequent sections.
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- corollary-1: body_md -->
\label{cor:power-from-spectral}
    Under Assumptions \ref{ass:basic} and \ref{ass:signal-init}, for every fixed integer $k\geq1$, every fixed $t\leq T$, and every integer $m\geq2$,
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:power-mse-universality-moments}
        \begin{aligned}
            \left\lVert \frac{1}{d}\mathrm{Tr}((\widehat S^t_\mathrm{RO})^k)-\frac{1}{d}\mathrm{Tr}((\widehat S^t_{\mathrm G})^k)\right\rVert_{L^m}
            &\leq C_{m,k}d^{-1/2},\\
            \left\lVert \frac{1}{d}\|\widehat S^t_\mathrm{RO}-S^\star\|_\mathrm{F}^2-\frac{1}{d}\|\widehat S^t_{\mathrm G}-S^\star\|_\mathrm{F}^2\right\rVert_{L^m}
            &\leq C_md^{-1/2}.
        \end{aligned}
    \end{equation}
    Both differences in \eqref{eq:power-mse-universality-moments} are also $\mathcal{O}_\prec(d^{-1/2})$. Moreover, if
    \begin{equation*}
        \widehat\nu_{\mathsf{E},t}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac1d\sum_{j=1}^d\delta_{\lambda_j(\widehat S_{\mathsf{E}}^t)},
    \end{equation*}
    then
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:esd-bl-universality}
        d_{\mathrm{BL}}(\widehat\nu_{\mathrm{RO},t},\widehat\nu_{\mathrm G,t})\xrightarrow[d\to\infty]{\mathbb P}0,
    \end{equation}
    where the bounded-Lipschitz distance is
    \begin{equation*}
        d_{\mathrm{BL}}(\nu,\nu')
        \mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\,\sup_{\substack{\|\varphi\|_\infty\,\leq\,1\\ \operatorname{Lip}(\varphi)\,\leq\,1}}
        \left|\int\varphi\,\mathrm d\nu-\int\varphi\,\mathrm d\nu'\right|.
    \end{equation*}
<!-- end corollary-1 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
The first bound is Theorem~\ref{thm:final-universality} with $\mu(x)=x^k$. For $\mathsf{E}\in\{\mathrm{RO},\mathrm G\}$,
    \begin{equation*}
        \frac1d\|\widehat S^t_{\mathsf{E}}-S^\star\|_\mathrm{F}^2
        =\frac1d\mathrm{Tr}((\widehat S^t_{\mathsf{E}})^2)-\frac2d\mathrm{Tr}(\widehat S^t_{\mathsf{E}}S^\star)+\frac1d\|S^\star\|_\mathrm{F}^2.
    \end{equation*}
    Subtracting the two identities cancels the signal-norm term, so the second bound follows from the triangle inequality, the first bound with $k=2$, and the signal-overlap comparison in Theorem~\ref{thm:final-universality}. The stochastic-domination claims follow from the all-moment bounds and Markov's inequality.

    For \eqref{eq:esd-bl-universality}, both empirical measures are supported on $[-C_\ast,C_\ast]$. The unit ball of bounded Lipschitz functions on this interval is compact in the uniform norm and admits, for every fixed accuracy, a finite net of smooth functions. Theorem~\ref{thm:final-universality} gives convergence in probability for every function in this finite net; approximation in the uniform norm then gives \eqref{eq:esd-bl-universality}.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- assumption-3: body_md -->
\label{ass:se-regularity}
    Suppose that $\alpha_d=n/p\to\alpha\in(0,\infty)$, and define the error maps
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathcal E_{0,d}(W)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\widehat S^0-S^\star,
        \qquad
        \mathcal E_{t,d}(W)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\sigma_{t-1}(S^\star+W)-S^\star,
        \quad 1\leq t\leq T.
    \end{equation*}
    For every $0\leq s,t\leq T$ and every positive semidefinite $K\in\mathbb R^{2\times2}$, let $(W_1,W_2)$ be jointly Gaussian symmetric matrices with upper-triangular entries independent across index pairs and
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathbb{E}[(W_a)_{jk}(W_b)_{jk}]
        =\frac{(1+\mathbf 1_{\{j=k\}})K_{ab}}{2(d+1)\alpha_d},
        \qquad a,b\in\{1,2\}.
    \end{equation*}
    Assume that the limit
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:se-regularity-limit}
        \lim_{d\to\infty}\frac2d\,\mathbb{E}\mathrm{Tr}\big(\mathcal E_{s,d}(W_1)\mathcal E_{t,d}(W_2)\big)
    \end{equation}
    exists and is finite.
<!-- end assumption-3 -->
<!-- proposition-1: body_md -->
\label{prop:gaussian-se}
    Let $S^\star$ and $\widehat S^0$ be deterministic sequences satisfying Assumptions \ref{ass:signal-init} and \ref{ass:se-regularity}, let Assumption~\ref{ass:basic} hold, and run the Gaussian recursion \eqref{eq:AMP-recursion} with the coefficients \eqref{eq:se-coefficients}. Then, for every $0\leq s,t\leq T$ and every $\mu\in\mathscr{C}^3(\mathbb R)$,
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:gaussian-se-observables}
        \Phi^{\mathrm{sig}}_{\mathrm G,t}-\phi^{\mathrm{sig}}_{d,t}\xrightarrow{\ \mathbb P\ }0,
        \qquad
        \Phi^{\mathrm{cross}}_{\mathrm G,s,t}-\phi^{\mathrm{cross}}_{d,s,t}\xrightarrow{\ \mathbb P\ }0,
        \qquad
        \Phi^{\mathrm{spec}}_{\mathrm G,t,\mu}-\phi^{\mathrm{spec}}_{d,t,\mu}\xrightarrow{\ \mathbb P\ }0.
    \end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-1 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{thm:se-transfer}
    Under the assumptions of Proposition~\ref{prop:gaussian-se}, the convergences \eqref{eq:gaussian-se-observables} hold with $\mathrm G$ replaced by $\mathrm{RO}$. In particular, for both ensembles and every $0\leq t\leq T$,
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:se-mse-limit}
        \frac1d\big\|\widehat S^t_{\mathsf{E}}-S^\star\big\|_\mathrm{F}^2
        \xrightarrow{\ \mathbb P\ }\frac12\,C_{t,t},
        \qquad \mathsf{E}\in\{\mathrm{RO},\mathrm G\}.
    \end{equation}
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
The first claim follows from Proposition~\ref{prop:gaussian-se} and \eqref{eq:transfer-principle} with $\phi_d$ equal to the corresponding prediction in \eqref{eq:se-predictions}. For \eqref{eq:se-mse-limit}, expand
    \begin{equation*}
        \frac1d\big\|\widehat S^t_{\mathsf{E}}-S^\star\big\|_\mathrm{F}^2
        =\Phi^{\mathrm{cross}}_{\mathsf{E},t,t}-2\Phi^{\mathrm{sig}}_{\mathsf{E},t}+\frac1d\|S^\star\|_\mathrm{F}^2,
        \qquad
        \phi^{\mathrm{cross}}_{d,t,t}-2\phi^{\mathrm{sig}}_{d,t}+\frac1d\|S^\star\|_\mathrm{F}^2
        =\frac1d\,\mathbb{E}\big\|\overline S^t-S^\star\big\|_\mathrm{F}^2,
    \end{equation*}
    and note that the right-hand side of the second identity converges to $\frac12C_{t,t}$ by \eqref{eq:se-arrays}.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- remark-2: body_md -->
\label{rem:se-interpretation}
    Write $\tau_t^2\mathrel{\mathop{:}}= Q_{t,t}=\Delta+C_{t,t}$. By \eqref{eq:se-noise-covariance}, the denoiser at step $t$ sees the signal corrupted by a GOE matrix, $\overline R^t=S^\star+W^t$, with $W^t$ distributed as $\sqrt{\tau_t^2\,d/(2(d+1)\alpha_d)}\,\widetilde Z_1$. Thus, in the normalization \eqref{eq:goe-entry-definition}, $W^t$ is asymptotically $\sqrt{\tau_t^2/(2\alpha)}\,\widetilde Z_1$. In the Bayes-optimal algorithm of \cite{MaillardAntoine2024Bloa}, $\sigma_t$ is the rotationally invariant estimator of \cite{BunAllezBouchaudPotters2016} for denoising $S^\star$ at this GOE noise level, and \eqref{eq:se-arrays} becomes a recursion for the matrix-denoising mean-squared error. Theorem~\ref{thm:se-transfer} does not cover that denoiser, which is neither bounded nor $C^4$; Section~\ref{subsec:numerics-spectra} examines it empirically.
<!-- end remark-2 -->
<!-- remark-3: body_md -->
\label{rem:se-regularity-sufficient}
    Assumption~\ref{ass:se-regularity} holds, for instance, whenever $\sup_d\|S^\star\|_\mathrm{op}<\infty$, the empirical spectral distribution of $S^\star$ converges weakly, and $\widehat S^0$ is a fixed continuous spectral function of $S^\star$ (for example, $\widehat S^0=0$). Represent a correlated Gaussian pair $(W_1,W_2)$ as linear combinations of independent GOE matrices. These GOE components and $S^\star$ are asymptotically free \cite[Section~5.4]{AndersonGuionnetZeitouni2010}, which gives convergence for normalized traces of polynomials. With probability at least $1-e^{-cd}$, the spectra of $S^\star+W_1$ and $S^\star+W_2$ lie in a fixed compact interval, on which the spectral maps can be approximated uniformly by polynomials; on the complementary event, boundedness of the spectral maps makes the contribution negligible. The same argument applies when $\widehat S^0$ is independent of $S^\star$, rotationally invariant, has uniformly bounded operator norm, and has an almost surely convergent empirical spectral distribution. The additional operator-norm bound on $S^\star$ is used only for these convenient sufficient conditions. The result itself assumes \eqref{eq:se-regularity-limit} directly.
<!-- end remark-3 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
Let $\Phi^{\mathsf E}$ denote any one of the three observables in the theorem, evaluated in ensemble $\mathsf E \in \{\mathrm{RO}, \mathrm{G}\}$. We first compare their conditional expectations given $\mathcal B_0$. Fix a realization of $(S^\star, \widehat{S}^0)$ satisfying Assumption~\ref{ass:signal-init}. Under the corresponding conditional law, the signal and initialization are deterministic, while the sensing columns and the observation noise retain the same laws and independence as before. Moreover, the proofs of Propositions~\ref{prop:remaining-quadratic} and \ref{prop:linear-comparison-complete} use $S^\star$ and $\widehat{S}^0$ only through the deterministic bounds in Assumption~\ref{ass:signal-init}. Their constants are therefore uniform over the realization of $\mathcal B_0$.
    We may consequently apply the Lindeberg argument in the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:reduction} with every expectation replaced by conditional expectation given $\mathcal B_0$. Since $n\leq C_0p=\mathcal{O}(d^2)$ and each one-column comparison is $\mathcal{O}(d^{-5/2})$, this gives
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:main-conditional-comparison}
        \left\lvert \mathbb{E}[\Phi^\mathrm{RO} \mid \mathcal B_0] - \mathbb{E}[\Phi^{\mathrm{G}} \mid \mathcal B_0]\right\rvert \le C_\mu d^{-1/2}
    \end{equation}
    almost surely. Here and below, the dependence on $\mu$ is omitted for the signal and cross-time observables.
    By Corollary~\ref{cor:concentration-goe-rank-one} and Theorem~\ref{thm:concentration-general-ensemble}, for every integer $m \ge 2$,
    \begin{equation}
        \left\lVert \Phi^{\mathsf E} - \mathbb{E}[\Phi^{\mathsf E} \mid \mathcal B_0]\right\rVert_{L^m} \,\le\, C_{m,\mu} d^{-1/2}, \qquad \mathsf E \,\in\, \{\mathrm{RO}, \mathrm{G}\}
    \end{equation}
    Combining these estimates with \eqref{eq:main-conditional-comparison} and the triangle inequality yields
    \begin{equation}
        \begin{aligned}
            \left\lVert \Phi^\mathrm{RO} - \Phi^{\mathrm{G}}\right\rVert_{L^m} &\le \left\lVert \Phi^\mathrm{RO} - \mathbb{E}[\Phi^\mathrm{RO} \mid \mathcal B_0]\right\rVert_{L^m} + \left\lVert \Phi^{\mathrm{G}} - \mathbb{E}[\Phi^{\mathrm{G}} \mid \mathcal B_0]\right\rVert_{L^m}\\
            &+ \left\lVert \mathbb{E}[\Phi^\mathrm{RO} \mid \mathcal B_0] - \mathbb{E}[\Phi^{\mathrm{G}} \mid \mathcal B_0]\right\rVert_{L^m}\\
            &\le C_{m,\mu} d^{-1/2}
        \end{aligned}
    \end{equation}
    Applying this to the three choices of $\Phi$ proves \eqref{eq:main-universality-moments}.
    Finally, fix $\varepsilon, D > 0$ and choose an integer $m \ge 2$ such that $m\varepsilon > D$. Markov's inequality gives
    \begin{equation}
        \mathbb P\left(\left\lvert \Phi^\mathrm{RO} - \Phi^{\mathrm{G}}\right\rvert \,>\, d^{-1/2 + \varepsilon}\right) \,\le\, C_{m,\mu}^m d^{-m\varepsilon} \,\le\, d^{-D}
    \end{equation}
    for all sufficiently large $d$. This proves \eqref{eq:main-universality-stochastic}.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- proposition-2: body_md -->
\label{prop:exact-loo}
    For $u \in \mathbb R^p$ and $t \leq T - 1$,
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:w-exact}
        w^t(u) = -\kappa_t K_i q^t(u) + \rho_t w^{t - 1}(u) + u \beta^t_i(u)
    \end{equation}
    where
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:beta-exact}
        \beta^t_i(u) \mathrel{\mathop{:}}= \kappa_t \langle u, {\mathbf s}^* - \widehat{\mathbf s}^t(u) \rangle + \nu_t \epsilon_i
    \end{equation}
    Furthermore, we have
    \begin{equation}
        q^{t + 1}(u) = G_t(y^t_i + m^t(u)) - G_t(y^t_i)
    \end{equation}
    where
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:loo-y-m-def}
        y^t_i \,\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\, \widehat{\mathbf s}^t_{\setminus i} + a_t h^t_{\setminus i} \,\in\, \mathbb R^p, \quad m^t(u) \,\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\, q^t(u) + a_t w^t(u) \,\in\, \mathbb R^p
    \end{equation}
    Consequently,
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:q-linearized}
        q^{t + 1}(u) = L^t_i m^t(u) + \varepsilon^t_i(u)
    \end{equation}
    where
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:L-epsilon-def}
        L^t_i \,\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\, D G_t(y^t_i):\mathbb R^p \,\to\, \mathbb R^p, \quad \|\varepsilon^t_i(u)\| \,\leq\, C \|m^t(u)\|^2
    \end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-2 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
Since $F(u) = F_{\setminus i} + u e_i^\top$ and $K_i=F_{\setminus i} F_{\setminus i}^\top$, we have $F(u)F(u)^\top=K_i+uu^\top$. Substituting into \eqref{eq:reduced-AMP-recursion} gives
    \begin{equation}
        h^t(u) = \kappa_t K_i({\mathbf s}^* - \widehat{\mathbf s}^t(u)) + \kappa_t uu^\top ({\mathbf s}^* - \widehat{\mathbf s}^t(u)) + \nu_t F_{\setminus i} \epsilon + \nu_t\epsilon_i u  + \rho_t h^{t-1}(u)
    \end{equation}
    where $h^t(u)$ is defined in \eqref{eq:h-def}. The leave-one-out field satisfies
    \begin{equation}
        h^t_{\setminus i} = \kappa_t K_i({\mathbf s}^* - \widehat{\mathbf s}^t_{\setminus i}) + \nu_t F_{\setminus i} \epsilon + \rho_t h^{t - 1}_{\setminus i}
    \end{equation}
    Subtracting gives
    \begin{equation}
        w^t(u) = -\kappa_t K_i q^t(u) + \rho_t w^{t - 1}(u) + u\left( \kappa_t \langle u, {\mathbf s}^* - \widehat{\mathbf s}^t(u) \rangle + \nu_t \epsilon_i \right)
    \end{equation}
    which proves the first two equations of the proposition. Next, we have
    \begin{equation}
        \widehat{\mathbf s}^{t +1}(u) \,=\, G_t(\widehat{\mathbf s}^t(u) + a_th^t(u)), \quad \widehat{\mathbf s}^{t + 1}_{\setminus i} \,=\, G_t(\widehat{\mathbf s}^t_{\setminus i} + a_t h^t_{\setminus i})
    \end{equation}
    Subtracting gives the third equation, with $y_i^t$ and $m^t(u)$ as in \eqref{eq:loo-y-m-def}. By Lemma \ref{lem:spectral-taylor} (spectral calculus) and the definition of $L_i^t$ in \eqref{eq:L-epsilon-def}, we have
    \begin{equation}
        \begin{aligned}
            \|\varepsilon^t_i(u)\| &= \|G_t(y^t_i + m^t(u)) - G_t(y^t_i) - D G_t(y^t_i)[m^t(u)]\| \\
            &\leq \frac{1}{2}\|\sigma''_t\|_\infty \|m^t(u)\|^2
        \end{aligned}
    \end{equation}
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- theorem-3: body_md -->
\label{thm:reduction}
    Under Assumptions \ref{ass:basic} and \ref{ass:signal-init}, suppose that for every $s, t \leq T$ and every leave-one-out index $i$, the following hold for some $C$ uniformly over $d$.
    \begin{enumerate}[label = (\roman*)]
        \item For every $\mathcal{G}_i$-measurable vector $\lambda_i \in \mathbb R^p$ with $\|\lambda_i\| \leq C_1 d^{-1/2}$ a.s.,
        \begin{equation}\label{eq:abstract-linear-prop}
            \left\lvert \mathbb{E}\big[\left\langle \lambda_i,q^t(f_i)\right\rangle-\left\langle \lambda_i,q^t(\widetilde f_i)\right\rangle\big]\right\rvert
            \le C d^{-5/2}.
        \end{equation}
        for some constant $C$ not dependent on $d$.
        \item For every $\mathcal{G}_i$-measurable symmetric matrix $H_i \in  \mathbb R^{p \times p}$ with $\|H_i\|_\mathrm{op} \leq C_2 d^{-1}$ a.s.,
        \begin{equation}\label{eq:abstract-quadratic-prop}
            \left|\mathbb{E}\langle q^t(f_i), H_i q^s(f_i) \rangle -  \mathbb{E}\langle q^t(\widetilde f_i), H_i q^s(\widetilde f_i) \rangle \right| \leq C d^{-5/2}
        \end{equation}
        for some constant $C$ not dependent on $d$.
    \end{enumerate}
    Then Theorem \ref{thm:final-universality} holds.
<!-- end theorem-3 -->
<!-- proposition-3: body_md -->
\label{prop:remaining-quadratic}
        Fix $s, t \leq T$. Let $H_i$ be a $\mathcal{G}_i$-measurable symmetric matrix with $\|H_i\|_\mathrm{op} \leq C_2 d^{-1}$. Then
        \begin{equation}
            \left|\mathbb{E}\langle q^t(f_i), H_i q^s(f_i) \rangle -  \mathbb{E}\langle q^t(\widetilde f_i), H_i q^s(\widetilde f_i) \rangle \right| \leq C d^{-5/2}
        \end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-3 -->
<!-- proposition-4: body_md -->
\label{prop:linear-comparison-complete}
    Fix $t \leq T$. Let $\lambda_i$ be any $\mathcal{G}_i$-measurable vector satisfying $\|\lambda_i\| \leq C_1d^{-1/2}$ a.s. Then
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:linear-comparison-complete}
        \left\lvert \mathbb{E}\big[\left\langle \lambda_i,q^t(f_i)\right\rangle-\left\langle \lambda_i,q^t(\widetilde f_i)\right\rangle\big]\right\rvert
        \le C d^{-5/2}.
    \end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-4 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label{lem:base-det-bounds}
    Under Assumptions \ref{ass:basic} and \ref{ass:signal-init}, for every $t \leq T$,
    \begin{equation}
        \|\widehat{\mathbf s}^t(F)\| \leq C \sqrt{d}, \quad \left\|\widehat{S}^t \right\|_\mathrm{op} \leq C
    \end{equation}
    For some $C = C(C_0, C_1)$. The same bound holds for the leave-one-out iterates $\widehat{\mathbf s}^t_{\setminus i}$ and $\widehat{S}^t_{\setminus i}$.
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
At $t=0$, the bounds follow from Assumption~\ref{ass:signal-init} and the isometry of $\operatorname{vec}$. For $t\geq1$, the matrices $\widehat{S}^t$ and $\widehat{S}^t_{\setminus i}$ are outputs of the spectral map $\sigma_{t-1}$, so their spectra are contained in $[-\|\sigma_{t-1}\|_\infty,\|\sigma_{t-1}\|_\infty]$. The lemma follows.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- proposition-5: body_md -->
\label{prop:delta-moments}
    For every integer $m\ge 1$ there is $C_m<\infty$ such that uniformly in $i$ and in $u\in\{f_i,\widetilde f_i\}$,
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:q-w-moment-bound}
        \max_{0\le t\le T}\left\lVert q^t(u)\right\rVert_{L^m}
        +
        \max_{0\le t\le T-1}\Big(\left\lVert w^t(u)\right\rVert_{L^m}+\left\lVert m^t(u)\right\rVert_{L^m}\Big)
        \le C_m d^{-1/2}.
    \end{equation}
    \begin{equation}
        \max_{0 \,\le\, t \,\le\, T - 1} \left\lVert \beta^t_i(u)\right\rVert_{L^m} \,\le\, C_m d^{-1/2}
    \end{equation}
    Moreover,
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:eps-moment-bound}
        \max_{0\,\le\, t\,\le\, T-1}\left\lVert \varepsilon_i^t(u)\right\rVert_{L^m}\,\le\, C_m d^{-1}.
    \end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-5 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
For any observable $\Phi$ of the AMP iterates, treat it as a function of the collection of sensing matrix, $S^\star$, the initialization $\widehat{S}^0$, and the observation noise $\epsilon$. Since the latter $3$ quantities are fixed for the purpose of universality proof, we write $\Phi(F), \Phi(\widetilde F), \Phi(F^{(i)})$ for the observables generated by Gaussian sensing, rank-one sensing, and interpolated matrices $F^{(i)}$ defined in \eqref{eq:interpolating-matrix-def}. Write telescoping sum
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:lindeberg-telescope}
        \mathbb{E}\Phi(F) - \mathbb{E} \Phi(\widetilde F) = \sum_{i = 1}^n \mathbb{E}\Phi(F^{(i)}) - \mathbb{E}\Phi(F^{(i + 1)})
    \end{equation}
    We treat each observable separately.
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item Correlation with signal. See that the change caused by column replacement is
        \begin{equation}
            \frac{1}{d} \left\langle {\mathbf s}^*,q^t(f_i)\right\rangle
        \end{equation}
        Because ${\mathbf s}^*$ is independent of $F,\widetilde F$, and $\|d^{-1} {\mathbf s}^*\| \leq Cd^{-1/2}$ based on Assumption \ref{ass:signal-init}, \eqref{eq:abstract-linear-prop} applies and we have
        \begin{equation}
            \left| \frac{1}{d} \mathbb{E}  \mathrm{Tr}(\widehat{S}^t(F^{(i)}) S^\star) - \frac{1}{d} \mathbb{E}\mathrm{Tr}(\widehat{S}^t(F^{(i + 1)}) S^\star) \right| \,=\, \left| \frac{1}{d} \mathbb{E} \left\langle {\mathbf s}^*,q^t(f_i)\right\rangle - \frac 1 d \mathbb{E} \left\langle {\mathbf s}^*,q^t(\widetilde f_i)\right\rangle \right| \,\leq\, Cd^{-5/2}
        \end{equation}
        Summing over $i$ in \eqref{eq:lindeberg-telescope} and using $n\leq C_0p=\mathcal{O}(d^2)$ gives the claimed $\mathcal{O}(d^{-1/2})$ bound.
        \item Cross-time correlation. Fix $0 \leq s, t \leq T$ and define
        \begin{equation}
            \Phi^\mathrm{cross}(A, B) = \frac{1}{d}\mathrm{Tr}(AB)
        \end{equation}
        Then for perturbations $\Delta_A,\Delta_B\in\textsf{Sym}_d$,
        \begin{equation}\label{eq:cross-exact-expansion}
            \Phi^{\mathrm{cross}}(A+\Delta_A,B+\Delta_B)-\Phi^{\mathrm{cross}}(A,B)
            =\frac1d\mathrm{Tr}(\Delta_A B)+\frac1d\mathrm{Tr}(A\Delta_B)+\frac1d\mathrm{Tr}(\Delta_A\Delta_B).
        \end{equation}
        So in the vectorized form with $A = \widehat{S}^t_{\setminus i}, B = \widehat{S}^s_{\setminus i}$, the increment is a sum of two linear forms of norms $\mathcal{O}(d^{-1/2})$ and a bilinear form with norm $\frac{1}{d}$. Apply \eqref{eq:cross-exact-expansion} with
        \begin{equation}
            A \,=\, \widehat{S}^t_{\setminus i}, \quad B \,=\, \widehat{S}^s_{\setminus i}, \quad \Delta_A \,=\, \text{mat}(q^t(u)), \quad \Delta_B \,=\, \mathrm{mat}(q^s(u))
        \end{equation}
        So we have
        \begin{equation}
            \begin{aligned}
                \frac 1 d \mathrm{Tr}(\widehat{S}^t(F(u)) \widehat{S}^s(u)) - \frac 1 d \mathrm{Tr}(\widehat{S}^t_{\setminus i} \widehat{S}^s_{\setminus i}) = \frac 1 d \left\langle q^t(u),\widehat{\mathbf s}^s_{\setminus i}\right\rangle + \frac{1}{d} \left\langle q^s(u),\widehat{\mathbf s}^t_{\setminus i}\right\rangle + \frac 1 d \left\langle q^t(u),q^s(u)\right\rangle
            \end{aligned}
        \end{equation}
        By Lemma \ref{lem:base-det-bounds}, $\|\widehat{\mathbf s}^t_{\setminus i}\|, \|\widehat{\mathbf s}^s_{\setminus i}\| \leq C \sqrt{d}$. So from hypothesis \eqref{eq:abstract-linear-prop}, we have
        \begin{equation}
            \left\lvert \frac 1 d \mathbb{E} \left\langle \widehat{\mathbf s}^t_{\setminus i},q^s(f_i)\right\rangle - \frac 1 d \mathbb{E} \left\langle \widehat{\mathbf s}^t_{\setminus i},q^s(\widetilde f_i)\right\rangle\right\rvert \leq C d^{-5/2}
        \end{equation}
        for any $t, s$. Further, with $H_i \mathrel{\mathop{:}}= \frac{1}{d}I_p$, so that $\|H_i\|_\mathrm{op} = \frac 1 d$. By hypothesis \eqref{eq:abstract-quadratic-prop}, we have
        \begin{equation}
            \left\lvert \frac 1 d \mathbb{E} \left\langle q^t(f_i),q^s(f_i)\right\rangle - \frac 1 d \mathbb{E} \left\langle q^t(\widetilde f_i),q^s(\widetilde f_i)\right\rangle\right\rvert \leq C d^{-5/2}
        \end{equation}
        Therefore, we have
        \begin{equation}
            \left\lvert \mathbb{E}\Phi^\mathrm{cross}(\widehat{S}^t(f_i), \widehat{S}^s( f_i)) - \mathbb{E}\Phi^\mathrm{cross}(\widehat{S}^t(\widetilde f_i), \widehat{S}^s( \widetilde f_i))\right\rvert \leq C d^{-5/2}
        \end{equation}
        Summing over $n\leq C_0p=\mathcal{O}(d^2)$ terms gives, for any $s,t\in[T]$,
        \begin{equation}
            \left\lvert \mathbb{E}[\Phi^\mathrm{cross}_{s,t}(F) - \Phi^\mathrm{cross}_{s,t}(\widetilde F)]\right\rvert = \mathcal{O}(d^{-1/2})
        \end{equation}
        as desired.
        \item Spectral Statistics. Fix $\mu\in\mathscr{C}^3(\mathbb R)$. Define
        \begin{equation}
            \Phi_\mu(S) \mathrel{\mathop{:}}= \frac{1}{d} \mathrm{Tr}(\mu(S))
        \end{equation}
        We expand the spectral statistics as follows. Let
        \begin{equation}
            A_i \mathrel{\mathop{:}}= \widehat{S}^t_{\setminus i}, \quad \Delta_i(u) \mathrel{\mathop{:}}= \text{mat}(q^t(u))
        \end{equation}
        Lemma \ref{lem:base-det-bounds} bounds the operator norms of both $A_i$ and $A_i+\Delta_i(u)$ uniformly. Applying Lemma \ref{lem:smooth-spectral-expansion} on this deterministic spectral interval gives
        \begin{equation}
            \Phi_\mu(A_i +\Delta_i(u)) - \Phi_\mu(A_i) = \frac 1 d \mathrm{Tr}(\mu'(A_i) \Delta_i(u)) + Q_{\mu, A_i}(\Delta_i(u)) + R_{\mu, A_i}(\Delta_i(u))
        \end{equation}
        where $d^{-1}\|\mu'(A_i)\|_\mathrm{F}\leq\|\mu'\|_{L^\infty(I_\ast)}d^{-1/2}$, so it satisfies \eqref{eq:abstract-linear-prop}. Furthermore, by Lemma \ref{lem:smooth-spectral-expansion}, the quadratic form $Q_{\mu,A_i}$ is representable by a symmetric matrix $K_{\mu, A_i}$ such that $\|K_{\mu, A_i}\|_\mathrm{op} \leq C_\mu d^{-1}$, so it satisfies \eqref{eq:abstract-quadratic-prop}. Finally, we have from Proposition \ref{prop:delta-moments} and the remainder bound from Lemma \ref{lem:smooth-spectral-expansion},
        \begin{equation}
            \mathbb{E}\left\lvert R_{\mu, A_i}(\Delta_i(u))\right\rvert \leq C_\mu d^{-1} \mathbb{E}\left\lVert q^t(u)\right\rVert^3 \leq C_\mu d^{-5/2}
        \end{equation}
        Therefore, the term in the telescoping sum has expectation at most $Cd^{-5/2}$. Summing over $n\leq C_0p=\mathcal{O}(d^2)$ terms gives the desired $\mathcal{O}(d^{-1/2})$ bound.
    \end{enumerate}
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label{lem:response-op-bounds}
    For every $m \ge 1$, there exists $C_m < \infty$ such that uniformly in $i$,
    \begin{equation}
        \max_{0 \,\le\, k \,<\, t \,\le\, T} \left\lVert \mathcal P_{k,t}\right\rVert_{L^m(\mathrm{op})} \,\le\, C_m, \quad \max_{t \,\le\, T} \left\lVert z^t_i(u)\right\rVert_{L^m} \,\le\, C_m d^{-1}
    \end{equation}
    uniformly in $u \in \{f_i, \widetilde f_i\}$
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
The operator $\mathcal P_{k,t}$ is a product of the block matrices $M_i^r$ in \eqref{eq:Mt-def}, followed by a projection. Each block has operator norm bounded by $C(1+\|K_i\|_\mathrm{op})$, since $\|L_i^t\|_\mathrm{op}=\|DG_t(y_i^t)\|_\mathrm{op}\leq\|\sigma_t'\|_\infty\leq C$. Assumption~\ref{ass:basic}, Lemma~\ref{lem:sensing-envelope}, and \eqref{eq:G-lipschitz} therefore give
    \begin{equation}
        \left\lVert M^t_i\right\rVert_\mathrm{op} \,\le\, C(1 + \left\lVert K_i\right\rVert_\mathrm{op}) \,\implies\, \left\lVert M^t_i\right\rVert_{L^m(\mathrm{op})} \,\le\, C_m
    \end{equation}
    The products contain at most $T$ factors. Since $T$ is fixed, their operator norms have moments of every order bounded uniformly in $d$, proving the first bound.

    For the second equation, we simply apply \eqref{eq:z-unrolled} and Minkowski to get
    \begin{equation}
        \begin{aligned}
            \left\lVert z^t_i(u)\right\rVert_{L^m} &\le\, \sum_{\ell \,=\, 1}^{t-1} \left\lVert \Pi_1 M^{t-1}_i \cdots M^{\ell + 1}_i \Pi_1^\top\right\rVert_{L^{2m}(\mathrm{op})} \left\lVert \varepsilon^\ell_i(u)\right\rVert_{L^{2m}} \\
            &\le\, C_m \sum_{k\,=\,0}^{t-1} \left\lVert \varepsilon^k_i(u)\right\rVert_{L^{2m}} \,\le\, C'_m d^{-1}
        \end{aligned}
    \end{equation}
    by Proposition \ref{prop:delta-moments}.
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- corollary-2: body_md -->
\label{cor:scalar-bounds}
    For every $m \ge 1$ there exists $C_m < \infty$ such that uniformly in $i$ and $u \in \{f_i, \widetilde f_i\}$,
    \begin{equation}
        \max_{0 \,\le\, t \,\le\, T - 1} \left\lVert \beta^t_i(u)\right\rVert_{L^m} \,\le\, C_m d^{-1/2}
    \end{equation}
    \begin{equation}
        \max_{0 \,\le\, t \,\le\, T - 1} \left\lVert \beta^t_{i,0}(u)\right\rVert_{L^m} \,\le\, C_m d^{-1/2}
    \end{equation}
    \begin{equation}
        \max_{0 \,\le\, k \,<\, t \,\le\, T - 1} \left\lVert \Omega_{k,t}(u)\right\rVert_{L^m} \,\le\, C_m, \quad \max_{0 \,\le\, t \,\le\, T - 1} \left\lVert \eta^t_i(u)\right\rVert_{L^m}\,\le\, C_m d^{-1}
    \end{equation}
<!-- end corollary-2 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
Proposition~\ref{prop:delta-moments} already bounds $\beta_i^t(u)$. For $\beta^t_{i,0}(u)$, \eqref{eq:beta0-def}, Lemma~\ref{lem:base-det-bounds}, Assumption~\ref{ass:signal-init}, and \eqref{eq:linear-moment-uncond} give
    \begin{equation}
        \left\lVert \left\langle u,{\mathbf s}^* - \widehat{\mathbf s}^t_{\setminus i}\right\rangle\right\rVert_{L^m}
        \le C_m d^{-1}\left\lVert {\mathbf s}^* - \widehat{\mathbf s}^t_{\setminus i}\right\rVert_{L^m}
        \le C_m d^{-1/2}.
    \end{equation}
    Since $\nu_t\epsilon_i=\mathcal{O}_{L^m}(d^{-1/2})$, this gives
    \begin{equation}
        \left\lVert \beta^t_{i,0}(u)\right\rVert_{L^m}\le C_m d^{-1/2}.
    \end{equation}
    For the $\Omega_{k,t}$, apply Cauchy--Schwarz
    \begin{equation}
        |\Omega_{k,t}| \,\le\, |\kappa_t| \left\lVert \mathcal P_{k,t}\right\rVert_\mathrm{op} \left\lVert u\right\rVert^2, \quad \left\lVert \Omega_{k,t}(u)\right\rVert_{L^m} \,\le\, \left\lvert \kappa_t\right\rvert \left\lVert \mathcal P_{k,t}\right\rVert_{L^{2m}(\mathrm{op})} \left\lVert u\right\rVert_{L^{4m}}^2
    \end{equation}
    which is bounded in $L^m$ uniformly in $d$ by Lemma \ref{lem:sensing-envelope} and Lemma \ref{lem:response-op-bounds}. By Cauchy--Schwarz again,
    \begin{equation}
        |\eta^t_i(u)| \,\le\, |\kappa_t| \left\lVert u\right\rVert \left\lVert z^t_i(u)\right\rVert, \quad \left\lVert \eta^t_i(u)\right\rVert_{L^m} \,\le\, \left\lvert \kappa_t\right\rvert \left\lVert u\right\rVert_{L^{2m}} \left\lVert z^t_i(u)\right\rVert_{L^{2m}}
    \end{equation}
    So the last bound is obtained by applying Lemma \ref{lem:response-op-bounds}.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- proposition-6: body_md -->
\label{prop:q-lin-error}
    For every $m \ge 1$ there exists $C_m < \infty$ such that uniformly in $i$ and $u \in \{f_i, \widetilde f_i\}$.
    \begin{equation}
        \max_{t \le T} \left\lVert q^t(u) - q^t_\mathrm{lin}(u)\right\rVert_{L^m} \le C_m d^{-1}
    \end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-6 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
Subtracting \eqref{eq:alpha-lin-system} from \eqref{eq:beta-kernel-recursion}, with $r^t(u) \mathrel{\mathop{:}}= \beta^t(u) - \alpha^t(u)$ distinct from the AMP residual $r^t$ in \eqref{eq:AMP-recursion}, we obtain
    \begin{equation}
        r^t(u) = -\sum_{k=0}^{t-1}\Omega_{k,t}(u) r^k(u) - \eta^t(u)
    \end{equation}
    Since $T$ is fixed and we have shown in Corollary \ref{cor:scalar-bounds} that $\Omega_{k,t}(u)$ have moments of all orders uniformly bounded in $d$, we have by Minkowski and induction,
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:r-scalar-bound}
        \max_{t \le T - 1} \left\lVert r^t(u)\right\rVert_{L^m} \le C_m d^{-1}
    \end{equation}
    Now subtract \eqref{eq:q-lin-def} from \eqref{eq:q-unrolled}, we have
    \begin{equation}
        q^t(u) - q ^t_\mathrm{lin}(u) = \sum_{k=0}^{t-1} \mathcal P_{k,t}u r^k(u) + z^t_i(u)
    \end{equation}
    Using Lemma \ref{lem:response-op-bounds}, Lemma \ref{lem:sensing-envelope}, and \eqref{eq:r-scalar-bound}, we get
    \begin{equation}
        \begin{aligned}
            \left\lVert q^t(u) - q^t_\mathrm{lin}(u)\right\rVert_{L^m} &\le\, \sum_{k\,=\,0}^{t-1} \left\lVert \mathcal P_{k,t}\right\rVert_{L^{3m}(\mathrm{op})} \left\lVert u\right\rVert_{L^{3m}} \left\lVert r^k(u)\right\rVert_{L^{3m}} + \left\lVert z^t_i(u)\right\rVert_{L^m} \\
            &\le C_m d^{-1}.
        \end{aligned}
    \end{equation}
    as desired.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
\label{lem:omega-fluctuations}
    For every $m \ge 1$, there exists $C_m < \infty$ such that uniformly in $i$, in $0 \le k < t \le T$, and in $u \in \{f_i, \widetilde f_i\}$,
    \begin{equation}
        \left\lVert \tilde{\Omega}_{k,t}(u)\right\rVert_{L^m} \le C_m d^{-1/2}
    \end{equation}
    and
    \begin{equation}
        \left\lVert \bar{\Omega}_{k,t}\right\rVert_{L^m} \le C_m
    \end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
By the definitions \eqref{eq:omega-def}, \eqref{eq:response-operator-def}, \eqref{eq:omega-bar-def}, and \eqref{eq:omega-fluctuation-def}, we have
    \begin{equation}
        \tilde{\Omega}_{k,t}(u) = \kappa_t \left( u^\top \mathcal P_{k,t}u - \frac 1 p  \mathrm{Tr} \mathcal P_{k,t} \right).
    \end{equation}
    Applying Corollary \ref{cor:one-column-moments} (ii) with $A = \mathcal P_{k,t}$ and using Lemma \ref{lem:response-op-bounds}, we get
    \begin{equation}
        \left\lVert \tilde{\Omega}_{k,t}(u)\right\rVert_{L^{m}} \le C_m \left\lVert \mathcal P_{k,t}\right\rVert_{L^{2m}(\mathrm{op})} d^{-1/2} \le C_m d^{-1/2}.
    \end{equation}
    Now we have
    \begin{equation}
        |\bar{\Omega}_{k,t}| \le |\kappa_t| \left\lVert \mathcal P_{k,t}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}},
    \end{equation}
    so
    \begin{equation}
        \left\lVert \bar{\Omega}_{k,t}\right\rVert_{L^m} \le |\kappa_t| \left\lVert \mathcal P_{k,t}\right\rVert_{L^m(\mathrm{op})} \le C_m
    \end{equation}
    by Lemma \ref{lem:response-op-bounds}.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- proposition-7: body_md -->
\label{prop:bar-alpha}
    For every fixed $t \le T - 1$ and every integer $m \ge 1$, the following holds uniformly in $i$ and in $u \in \{f_i, \widetilde f_i\}$.
    \begin{enumerate}[label = (\roman*)]
        \item There exist $\mathcal{G}_i$-measurable coefficients $\{c_{t,r}\}_{r = 0}^t$ such that
        \begin{equation}\label{eq:bar-alpha-linear-combo}
            \bar \alpha^t(u) \,=\, \sum_{r\,=\,0}^t c_{t,r} \beta^r_{i,0}(u), \quad c_{t,t} \,=\, 1
        \end{equation}
        and
        \begin{equation}\label{eq:c-coeff-bound}
            \max_{r \le t} \left\lVert c_{t,r}\right\rVert_{L^m} \le C_m
        \end{equation}
        \item The error
        \begin{equation}\label{eq:d-def}
            d^t(u) \mathrel{\mathop{:}}= \beta^t(u) - \bar\alpha^t(u)
        \end{equation}
        is bounded by
        \begin{equation}\label{eq:d-bound}
            \left\lVert d^t(u)\right\rVert_{L^m} \le C_m d^{-1}
        \end{equation}
    \end{enumerate}
<!-- end proposition-7 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
We prove (i) by induction on $t$. Since $\bar\alpha^0(u)=\beta^0_{i,0}(u)$, we take $c_{0,0}=1$. Assume \eqref{eq:bar-alpha-linear-combo} holds for all $r<t$. Expanding the recursion gives
    \begin{equation}
        \begin{aligned}
            \bar\alpha^t(u) &=\, \beta^t_{i,0}(u) - \sum_{k\,=\,0}^{t-1} \bar{\Omega}_{k,t} \sum_{r\,=\,0}^k c_{k,r} \beta^r_{i,0}(u) \\
            &=\, \beta^t_{i,0}(u) + \sum_{r\,=\,0}^{t-1} \left(- \sum_{k\,=\,r}^{t-1} \bar{\Omega}_{k,t} c_{k,r} \right)\beta^r_{i,0}(u)
        \end{aligned}
    \end{equation}
    But observe that $\left(- \sum_{k=r}^{t-1} \bar{\Omega}_{k,t} c_{k,r} \right)$ is $\mathcal{G}_i$-measurable. Therefore, \eqref{eq:bar-alpha-linear-combo} holds with
    \begin{equation}
        c_{t,t} \,=\, 1, \quad c_{t,r} \,=\, - \sum_{k \,=\, r}^{t-1} \bar{\Omega}_{k,t} c_{k,r} \quad (0 \,\le\, r \,<\, t)
    \end{equation}
    Since $T$ is fixed, the moment bounds on $\bar{\Omega}_{k,t}$ in Lemma~\ref{lem:omega-fluctuations} and repeated applications of H\"older's and Minkowski's inequalities give \eqref{eq:c-coeff-bound}.

    To prove (ii), we subtract the $\mathcal{G}_i$-measurable approximation \eqref{eq:bar-alpha-linear-combo} from the exact recursion \eqref{eq:beta-kernel-recursion} to get
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:d-recursion}
        d^t(u) \,=\, - \sum_{k\,=\,0}^{t-1} \bar{\Omega}_{k,t} d^k(u) - \sum_{k \,=\, 0}^{t - 1} \tilde{\Omega}_{k,t}(u)\beta^k_i(u) - \eta^t_i(u)
    \end{equation}
    At $t=0$, the right-hand side is zero. Assume \eqref{eq:d-bound} holds for all $k<t$. Lemma~\ref{lem:omega-fluctuations} and Corollary~\ref{cor:scalar-bounds} give
    \begin{equation}
        \left\lVert  \sum_{k\,=\,0}^{t-1} \tilde{\Omega}_{k,t}(u)\beta^k_i(u) \right\rVert_{L^m} \,\le\, \sum_{k\,=\,0}^{t-1} \left\lVert \tilde{\Omega}_{k,t}(u)\right\rVert_{L^{2m}} \left\lVert \beta^k_i(u)\right\rVert_{L^{2m}} \,\le\, C_m d^{-1}
    \end{equation}
    and
    \begin{equation}
        \left\lVert \eta^t_i(u)\right\rVert_{L^m} \le C_m d^{-1}
    \end{equation}
    Applying Minkowski's and H\"older's inequalities to \eqref{eq:d-recursion}, using the fixed horizon and the uniform moment bounds for $\bar{\Omega}_{k,t}$, gives
    \begin{equation}
        \left\lVert d^t(u)\right\rVert_{L^m} \,\le\, C_m d^{-1} + C_m \sum_{k\,=\,0}^{t-1} \left\lVert d^k(u)\right\rVert_{L^{2m}} \,\le\, C_m d^{-1}
    \end{equation}
    so the claim follows from induction.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- corollary-3: body_md -->
\label{cor:bar-q}
    For every $t \le T$,
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:q-minus-barq-decomp}
        q^t(u) - \bar q^t(u) = \sum_{k=0}^{t-1} \mathcal P_{k,t} u\  d^k(u) + z^t_i(u)
    \end{equation}
    So for every integer $m \ge 1$, we have uniformly in $i$ and $u \in \{f_i, \widetilde f_i\}$,
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:q-minus-barq}
        \left\lVert q^t(u) - \bar q^t(u)\right\rVert_{L^m} \le C_m d^{-1}
    \end{equation}
    Moreover, there exist $\mathcal{G}_i$-measurable matrices $(\mathcal B_{t,r})_{r = 0}^{t-1} : \mathbb R^p \to \mathbb R^p$ such that
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:bar-q-linear-combo}
        \bar q^t(u) = \sum_{r = 0}^{t-1} \mathcal B_{t,r} u \beta^r_{i,0}(u)
    \end{equation}
    and satisfies
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:B-operator-bound}
        \max_{r \le t - 1} \left\lVert \mathcal B_{t,r}\right\rVert_{L^m(\mathrm{op})} \le C_m
    \end{equation}
<!-- end corollary-3 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
Subtract \eqref{eq:bar-q-def} from the exact formula \eqref{eq:q-unrolled}, we obtain
    \begin{equation}
        q^t(u) - \bar q^t(u) = \sum_{k=0}^{t-1} \mathcal P_{k,t} u\  d^k(u) + z^t_i(u)
    \end{equation}
    By Proposition \ref{prop:bar-alpha}, Lemma \ref{lem:response-op-bounds}, Lemma \ref{lem:sensing-envelope}, and the bound on $z^t_i(u)$ from Lemma \ref{lem:response-op-bounds}, we have from H\"older,
    \begin{equation}
        \begin{aligned}
            \left\lVert q^t(u) - \bar q^t(u)\right\rVert_{L^m} &\le\, \sum_{k\,=\,0}^{t-1}\left\lVert \mathcal P_{k,t}\right\rVert_{L^{3m}(\mathrm{op})} \left\lVert u\right\rVert_{L^{3m}} \left\lVert d^k(u)\right\rVert_{L^{3m}} + \left\lVert z^t_i(u)\right\rVert_{L^m} \\
            &\le C_m d^{-1}
        \end{aligned}
    \end{equation}
    so \eqref{eq:q-minus-barq} is shown. Now plug the formulation of $\bar \alpha^t$ in \eqref{eq:bar-alpha-linear-combo} of Proposition \ref{prop:bar-alpha} into the definition of $\bar q^t$ in \eqref{eq:bar-q-def}, we have
    \begin{equation}
        \begin{aligned}
            \bar q^t(u) \,=\, \sum_{k\,=\,0}^{t-1}\sum_{r\,=\,0}^k \mathcal P_{k,t} u \ c_{k,r} \beta^r_{i,0}(u) \,=\, \sum_{r\,=\,0}^{t-1} \left( \sum_{k\,=\,r}^{t-1} c_{k,r} \mathcal P_{k,t} \right) u \beta^r_{i,0}(u)
        \end{aligned}
    \end{equation}
    Therefore, \eqref{eq:bar-q-linear-combo} holds with
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:Btr-def}
        \mathcal B_{t,r} \mathrel{\mathop{:}}= \sum_{k=r}^{t-1} c_{k,r}\mathcal P_{k,t}.
    \end{equation}
    The $L^m$ bound on the operator norm follows by triangle inequality, Proposition \ref{prop:bar-alpha}, and Lemma \ref{lem:response-op-bounds}.
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
We first show that the quadratic form is well-approximated by the $\mathcal{G}_i$-linearized replacement. In particular, by Corollary \ref{cor:bar-q}, Proposition \ref{prop:delta-moments}, and the hypothesis that $\left\lVert H_i\right\rVert_\mathrm{op} \le Cd^{-1}$, we have from H\"older
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:quad-error-r1}
        \begin{aligned}
            \mathbb{E} \left\lvert \left\langle q^t(u),H_i q^s(u)\right\rangle - \left\langle \bar q^t(u),H_i \bar q^s(u)\right\rangle \right\rvert &\le \left\lVert H_i\right\rVert_\mathrm{op} \bigg( \left\lVert q^t(u) - \bar q^t(u)\right\rVert_{L^2} \left\lVert q^s(u)\right\rVert_{L^2} \\
            & \ + \left\lVert \bar q^t(u)\right\rVert_{L^2} \left\lVert q^s(u) - \bar q^s(u)\right\rVert_{L^2} \bigg) \\
            &\le Cd^{-5/2}
        \end{aligned}
    \end{equation}
    uniformly in $u \in \{f_i, \widetilde f_i\}$ because both $q^r(u)$ and $\bar q^r(u)$ are $\mathcal{O}_{L^2}(d^{-1/2})$ while $q^r(u) - \bar q^r(u) = \mathcal{O}_{L^2}(d^{-1})$. Therefore, it suffices to compare $\left\langle \bar q^t(u),H_i \bar q^s(u)\right\rangle$ over $u \in \{f_i, \widetilde f_i\}$. By expansion \eqref{eq:bar-q-linear-combo}, we have
    \begin{equation}
        \bar q^t(u)=\sum_{r=0}^{t-1}\mathcal B_{t,r}u\ \beta_{i,0}^r(u),
        \qquad
        \bar q^s(u)=\sum_{\ell=0}^{s-1}\mathcal B_{s,\ell}u\ \beta_{i,0}^{\ell}(u).
    \end{equation}
    So we can write
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:barq-quad-expansion}
        \left\langle \bar q^t(u),H_i \bar q^s(u)\right\rangle \,=\, \sum_{r\,=\,0}^{t-1} \sum_{\ell\,=\,0}^{s-1} \beta^r_{i,0}(u) \beta^\ell_{i,0}(u) \left\langle u,M_{t,s,r,\ell} u\right\rangle
    \end{equation}
    where $M_{t,s,r,\ell} = \mathcal B_{t,r}^\top H_i \mathcal B_{s,\ell}$, with $\mathcal B_{t,r}$ defined in \eqref{eq:Btr-def}. By \eqref{eq:B-operator-bound}, the matrices $\mathcal B_{t,r}$ have moments bounded uniformly in $d$. Hence, for any $m \ge 1$,
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:M-op-bound}
        \max_{t,s,r,\ell} \left\lVert M_{t,s,r,\ell}\right\rVert_{L^m(\mathrm{op})} \le C_m d^{-1}
    \end{equation}
    Since $M_{t,s,r,\ell}$ is $\mathcal{G}_i$-measurable and $u$ is isotropic, we again approximate the quadratic form by the trace. Write
    \begin{equation}
        u^\top M_{t,s,r,\ell} u \,=\, \frac 1 p \mathrm{Tr} M_{t,s,r,\ell} + \tilde m_{t,s,r,\ell}(u), \quad \tilde m_{t,s,r,\ell}(u) \,\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\, u^\top M_{t,s,r,\ell} u - \frac 1 p \mathrm{Tr} M_{t,s,r,\ell}
    \end{equation}
    Plugging this into \eqref{eq:barq-quad-expansion} gives
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:barq-leading-split}
        \left\langle \bar q^t(u),H_i \bar q^s(u)\right\rangle = T(u) + R(u)
    \end{equation}
    where
    \begin{equation}
        T(u) \,\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\, \frac 1 p \sum_{r \,=\, 0}^{t - 1} \sum_{\ell \,=\, 0}^{s - 1} \mathrm{Tr}(M_{t,s,r,\ell}) \beta^r_{i,0}(u) \beta^\ell_{i,0}(u), \quad R(u) \,\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\, \sum_{r \,=\, 0}^{t - 1} \sum_{\ell \,=\, 0}^{s - 1} \tilde m_{t,s,r,\ell}(u)\beta^r_{i,0}(u) \beta^\ell_{i,0}(u)
    \end{equation}
    To compare the $T$ term, we condition on $\mathcal{G}_i$. Recall, $a^r_i \mathrel{\mathop{:}}= {\mathbf s}^* - \widehat{\mathbf s}^r_{\setminus i} \in \mathbb R^p, \ \ \beta^{r}_{i,0} = \kappa_r \left\langle u,a^r_i\right\rangle +\nu_r\epsilon_i$. Therefore, by Lemma \ref{lem:one-column-moments}, we have
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:betar-betal-condition-on-Gi}
        \mathbb{E}[\beta^r_{i,0}(u) \beta^\ell_{i,0}(u) \ | \ \mathcal{G}_i] = \frac{\kappa_r \kappa_\ell}{p} \left\langle a^r_i,a^\ell_i\right\rangle + \nu_r \nu_\ell \epsilon_i^2
    \end{equation}
    This conditional expectation is the same for $f_i$ and $\widetilde f_i$. Taking expectations gives
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:trace-part-equal}
        \mathbb{E}[T(f_i) | \mathcal{G}_i] \,=\, \mathbb{E}[T(\widetilde f_i) | \mathcal{G}_i]  \,\implies\, \mathbb{E}[T(f_i) - T(\widetilde f_i)] \,=\, 0
    \end{equation}
    Now we bound the remainder for the quadratic form. By Corollary \ref{cor:scalar-bounds}, we have $\left\lVert \beta^r_{i,0}(u)\right\rVert_{L^4} \le Cd^{-1/2}$. So by \eqref{eq:M-op-bound} and Corollary \ref{cor:one-column-moments}, we have $\left\lVert \tilde m_{t,s,r,\ell}\right\rVert_{L^2} \le Cd^{-3/2}$. Therefore, by H\"older,
    \begin{equation}
        \mathbb{E}|R(u)| \,\le\, \sum_{r \,=\, 0}^{t-1} \sum_{\ell\,=\,0}^{s-1} \left\lVert \beta^r_{i,0}(u)\right\rVert_{L^4} \left\lVert \beta^\ell_{i,0}(u)\right\rVert_{L^4} \left\lVert \tilde m_{t,s,r,\ell}\right\rVert_{L^2} \,\le\, Cd^{-5/2}
    \end{equation}
    uniformly in $u \in \{f_i, \widetilde f_i\}$. Together with \eqref{eq:barq-leading-split} and\eqref{eq:trace-part-equal}, we have
    \begin{equation}
        \left\lvert \mathbb{E}\left\langle \bar q^t(f_i),H_i \bar q^s(f_i)\right\rangle  - \mathbb{E}\left\langle \bar q^t(\widetilde f_i),H_i \bar q^s(\widetilde f_i)\right\rangle  \right\rvert \le Cd^{-5/2}
    \end{equation}
    Together with \eqref{eq:quad-error-r1}, this proves the proposition.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- proposition-8: body_md -->
\label{prop:leading-linear-barq}
    Fix $t \le T$. Let $\lambda_i$ be any $\mathcal{G}_i$-measurable vector. Then
    \begin{equation}
        \mathbb{E}[\left\langle \lambda_i,\bar q^t(f_i)\right\rangle | \mathcal{G}_i] = \mathbb{E}[ \left\langle \lambda_i,\bar q^t(\widetilde f_i)\right\rangle | \mathcal{G}_i], \quad a.s.
    \end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-8 -->
<!-- proposition-9: body_md -->
\label{prop:remaining-linear}
    Fix $t \le T$. Let $\lambda_i$ be any $\mathcal{G}_i$-measurable vector satisfying $\left\lVert \lambda_i\right\rVert \le C d^{-1/2}$ almost surely. Then
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:linear-remainder-reduction}
        \left\lvert \mathbb{E}\big[\left\langle \lambda_i,q^t(f_i)\right\rangle - \left\langle \lambda_i,q^t(\widetilde f_i)\right\rangle\big]\right\rvert \le \left\lvert \mathbb{E}\big[\left\langle \lambda_i,z_i^t(f_i)\right\rangle - \left\langle \lambda_i,z_i^t(\widetilde f_i)\right\rangle\big]\right\rvert + C d^{-5/2}.
    \end{equation}
    In particular, if the projected nonlinear remainder satisfies
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:projected-z-needed}
        \left\lvert \mathbb{E}\big[\left\langle \lambda_i,z_i^t(f_i)\right\rangle - \left\langle \lambda_i,z_i^t(\widetilde f_i)\right\rangle\big]\right\rvert \le C d^{-5/2},
    \end{equation}
    then
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:remaining-linear-conclusion}
        \left\lvert \mathbb{E}\big[\left\langle \lambda_i,q^t(f_i)\right\rangle - \left\langle \lambda_i,q^t(\widetilde f_i)\right\rangle\big]\right\rvert \le C d^{-5/2}.
    \end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-9 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
\label{lem:m-representation}
    For every fixed $t \leq T - 1$,
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:m-exact-unrolled}
        m^t(u) = \zeta^t(u) + \sum_{k=0}^t \mathcal M_{k,t} u \beta^k_i(u)
    \end{equation}
    Moreover, for every $m \ge 1$, we have
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:m-response-op-bound}
        \max_{k\le t} \left\lVert \mathcal M_{k,t}\right\rVert_{L^m(\mathrm{op})} \le C_m
    \end{equation}
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:zeta-exact-bound}
        \left\lVert \zeta^t(u)\right\rVert_{L^m} \le C_m d^{-1}
    \end{equation}
    uniformly in $u \in \{f_i, \widetilde f_i\}$. Define also the $\mathcal{G}_i$ approximation for $m^t(u)$, with $\bar\alpha^k(u)$ from \eqref{eq:bar-alpha-recursion},
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:mbar-def}
        \bar m^t(u) \mathrel{\mathop{:}}= \sum_{k=0}^t \mathcal M_{k,t}u \bar \alpha^k(u)
    \end{equation}
    Then there exist $\mathcal{G}_i$-measurable operators $(\mathcal R_{t,r})_{r = 0}^t:\mathbb R^p \to \mathbb R^p$ such that
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:mbar-linear-combo}
        \bar m^t(u) \,=\, \sum_{r\,=\,0}^t \mathcal R_{t,r}u \beta^r_{i,0}(u), \quad \max_{r \,\le\, t} \left\lVert \mathcal R_{t,r}\right\rVert_{L^m(\mathrm{op})} \,\le\, C_m
    \end{equation}
    With $d^k(u)$ from \eqref{eq:d-def} of Proposition \ref{prop:bar-alpha}, define the unbolded remainder
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:s-remainder-def}
        s^t(u) \mathrel{\mathop{:}}= \sum_{k=0}^t \mathcal M_{k,t} u\ d^k(u).
    \end{equation}
    Then we have
    \begin{align}
        m^t(u) - \bar m^t(u) &= s^t(u) + \zeta^t(u) \label{eq:m-minus-mbar-decomp} \\
        \left\lVert \bar m^t(u)\right\rVert_{L^m} &\le C_m d^{-1/2} \label{eq:mbar-size} \\
        \left\lVert s^t(u)\right\rVert_{L^m} &\le C_m d^{-1}\label{eq:s-remainder-bound}
    \end{align}
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- corollary-4: body_md -->
\label{cor:epsilon-third-order-vector}
    Fix $i\in[n]$ and $0\le k\le T-1$. Let $\varepsilon_i^k(u)$ be as
    defined in \eqref{eq:q-linearized} of Proposition \ref{prop:exact-loo}. Let
    \begin{equation}
        y^k_i \mathrel{\mathop{:}}= \widehat{\mathbf s}^k_{\setminus i}+a_kh^k_{\setminus i}
    \end{equation}
    as in Proposition \ref{prop:exact-loo}. Define
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:vector-local-def}
        \mathbf B_i^k(g_1,g_2) \mathrel{\mathop{:}}= \mathbf B_{k,y^k_i}(g_1,g_2), \qquad
        \mathbf T_i^k(g_1,g_2,g_3) \mathrel{\mathop{:}}= \mathbf T_{k,y^k_i}(g_1,g_2,g_3),
    \end{equation}
    and
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:vector-local-remainder-def}
        \rho_{i,k}^{(4)}(g) \mathrel{\mathop{:}}= \rho_{k,y^k_i}^{(4)}(g).
    \end{equation}
    Then for every $u\in\mathbb R^p$,
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:epsilon-third-order-vector}
        \varepsilon_i^k(u) = \mathbf B_i^k(m^k(u),m^k(u)) + \mathbf T_i^k(m^k(u),m^k(u),m^k(u))
        +
        \rho_{i,k}^{(4)}(m^k(u)).
    \end{equation}
    Moreover, there is a constant $C<\infty$, depending only on
    $M_{\sigma,4}$ from \eqref{eq:M-sigma-4-def} in Lemma \ref{lem:spectral-taylor-4}, such that for all $g,g_1,g_2,g_3,w\in\mathbb R^p$,
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:vector-local-bound}
        \left\lVert \mathbf B_i^k(g_1,g_2)\right\rVert \le C\left\lVert g_1\right\rVert\left\lVert g_2\right\rVert,
        \qquad
        \left\lVert \mathbf T_i^k(g_1,g_2,g_3)\right\rVert \le C\left\lVert g_1\right\rVert\left\lVert g_2\right\rVert\left\lVert g_3\right\rVert,
        \qquad
        \left\lVert \rho_{i,k}^{(4)}(g)\right\rVert \le C\left\lVert g\right\rVert^4,
    \end{equation}
    and
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:scalarized-local-B-HS}
        \left\|\left\langle w,\mathbf B_i^k(\cdot,\cdot)\right\rangle\right\|_\mathrm{HS}
        \le C\sqrt d\left\lVert w\right\rVert,
        \qquad
        \left\|\left\langle w,\mathbf T_i^k(\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)\right\rangle\right\|_\mathrm{HS}
        \le Cd\left\lVert w\right\rVert.
    \end{equation}
<!-- end corollary-4 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
By the definition of $\varepsilon_i^k(u)$ in \eqref{eq:q-linearized} of Proposition \ref{prop:exact-loo}, with $G_k$ defined in \eqref{eq:vectorized-AMP-recursion},
    \begin{equation}
        \varepsilon_i^k(u) = G_k(y_i^k+m^k(u))-G_k(y_i^k)-DG_k(y_i^k)m^k(u).
    \end{equation}
    Applying Lemma \ref{lem:spectral-taylor-4} with $t=k$, $y=y_i^k$, and
    $g=m^k(u)$ gives \eqref{eq:epsilon-third-order-vector}. The bounds
    \eqref{eq:vector-local-bound} and \eqref{eq:scalarized-local-B-HS} are \eqref{eq:local-spectral-B-bound}--\eqref{eq:local-spectral-T-HS} of Lemma \ref{lem:spectral-taylor-4}
    under the definitions in \eqref{eq:vector-local-def}.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
\label{lem:projected-z-representation}
    For every $0 \le k \le t - 1$ and every integer $m \ge 1$,
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:projected-z-unrolled}
        \left\langle \lambda_i,z^t_i(u)\right\rangle = \sum_{k=0}^{t-1} \left\langle \vartheta_{k,t},\varepsilon^k_i(u)\right\rangle
    \end{equation}
    and we also have
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:vartheta-bound}
        \left\lVert \vartheta_{k,t}\right\rVert_{L^m} \le C_m d^{-1/2}
    \end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
The product $\left( M_i^{t-1} \cdots M_i^{k + 1} \right)^\top$, with $M_i^r$ as in \eqref{eq:Mt-def}, has operator norm moments bounded uniformly in $d$ by Lemma \ref{lem:response-op-bounds}. Since $\left\lVert \lambda_i\right\rVert \le Cd^{-1/2}$, this gives \eqref{eq:vartheta-bound}. Equation \eqref{eq:projected-z-unrolled} is a direct consequence of \eqref{eq:vartheta-use} and the definition \eqref{eq:z-unrolled}.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- lemma-6: body_md -->
\label{lem:epsilon-quad-lin}
    For $0 \le k \le T-1$, define
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:epsilon-quad-lin-def}
        \varepsilon^k_{i,\mathrm{quad}, \mathrm{lin}}(u) \mathrel{\mathop{:}}= \mathbf B^k(\bar m^k(u), \bar m^k(u))
    \end{equation}
    Then for every $m \ge 1$,
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:epsilon-quad-lin-size}
        \left\lVert \varepsilon^k_{i,\mathrm{quad},\mathrm{lin}}(u)\right\rVert_{L^m} \le C_m d^{-1}
    \end{equation}
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:epsilon-quad-lin-error}
        \left\lVert \varepsilon^k_i(u) - \varepsilon^k_{i,\mathrm{quad},\mathrm{lin}}(u) \right\rVert_{L^m} \le C_m d^{-3/2}
    \end{equation}
    uniformly for $u \in \{f_i, \widetilde f_i\}$
<!-- end lemma-6 -->
<!-- corollary-5: body_md -->
\label{cor:zeta-quad-lin}
    For every fixed $k\le T$ and every integer $m\ge 1$,
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:U-response-bound}
        \max_{0\,\le\, \ell\,<\,k}\left\lVert \mathcal U_{\ell,k}\right\rVert_{L^m(\mathrm{op})}\,\le\, C_m,
    \end{equation}
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:zeta-quad-lin-size}
        \left\lVert \zeta_{\mathrm{quad},\mathrm{lin}}^k(u)\right\rVert_{L^m}\le C_m d^{-1},
    \end{equation}
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:zeta-quad-lin-error}
        \left\lVert \tilde \zeta^k(u)\right\rVert_{L^m}\le C_m d^{-3/2},
    \end{equation}
    uniformly in $u\in\{f_i,\tilde  f_i\}$.
<!-- end corollary-5 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
The operator bound \eqref{eq:U-response-bound} follows from the definition of $\mathcal U_{\ell,k}$ in \eqref{eq:U-propagator-def}, the definition of $\Gamma_k$ in \eqref{eq:Gamma-def}, the block matrices $M_i^r$ in \eqref{eq:Mt-def}, and the bounds in Lemma \ref{lem:sensing-envelope}, \eqref{eq:m-response-op-bound} of Lemma \ref{lem:m-representation}, and Lemma \ref{lem:response-op-bounds}. Then \eqref{eq:zeta-quad-lin-size} is immediate from \eqref{eq:epsilon-quad-lin-size} of Lemma \ref{lem:epsilon-quad-lin}. Finally,
    \begin{equation}
        \tilde\zeta^k(u)
        =\sum_{\ell=0}^{k-1} \mathcal U_{\ell,k}\big(\varepsilon_i^\ell(u)-\varepsilon_{i,\mathrm{quad},\mathrm{lin}}^\ell(u)\big),
    \end{equation}
    so \eqref{eq:zeta-quad-lin-error} follows from \eqref{eq:epsilon-quad-lin-error} of Lemma \ref{lem:epsilon-quad-lin}.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- proposition-10: body_md -->
\label{prop:projected-z-cubic-quartic}
    For every $t\le T$, the cubic and quartic contributions are small in both
    the GOE and rank-one cases. More precisely,
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:I34-individual-bounds}
        \max_{u\in\{f_i,\widetilde f_i\}}
        \left\{
            \left\lvert \mathbb{E}\!\left[\mathrm{I}_t^{(3)}(u)\right]\right\rvert + \left\lvert \mathbb{E}\!\left[\mathrm{I}_t^{(4)}(u)\right]\right\rvert
        \right\} \le C d^{-5/2}.
    \end{equation}
    Consequently, by the triangle inequality,
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:I34-comparison}
        \left\lvert \mathbb{E}\!\left[\mathrm{I}_t^{(3)}(f_i)-\mathrm{I}_t^{(3)}(\widetilde f_i)\right]\right\rvert + \left\lvert \mathbb{E}\!\left[\mathrm{I}_t^{(4)}(f_i)-\mathrm{I}_t^{(4)}(\widetilde f_i)\right]\right\rvert \le C d^{-5/2},
    \end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-10 -->
<!-- proposition-11: body_md -->
\label{prop:projected-z-quad-reduction}
    For every fixed $t \le T$, with $\mathrm{I}_t^{(2,\mathrm{lin})}$ and $\mathrm{I}_t^{(\mathrm{fb},\mathrm{main})}$ as defined in \eqref{eq:quadratic-main-terms}, we have
    \begin{equation}
        \left\lvert 
        \mathbb{E}[\mathrm{I}^{(2)}_t(f_i) - \mathrm{I}^{(2)}_t(\widetilde f_i)] - \mathbb{E}[\mathrm{I}^{(2,\mathrm{lin})}_t(f_i) - \mathrm{I}^{(2,\mathrm{lin})}_t(\widetilde f_i)] - \mathbb{E}[\mathrm{I}^{(\mathrm{fb}, \mathrm{main})}_t(f_i) - \mathrm{I}^{(\mathrm{fb}, \mathrm{main})}_t(\widetilde f_i)]
        \right\rvert \le C d^{-5/2}
    \end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-11 -->
<!-- proposition-12: body_md -->
\label{prop:projected-z-quad-lin-comp}
    For every $t \le T$, we have
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:I2lin-comp}
        \left\lvert \mathbb{E}[\mathrm{I}^{(2,\mathrm{lin})}_t(f_i) - \mathrm{I}^{(2,\mathrm{lin})}_t(\widetilde f_i)]\right\rvert \le C d^{-5/2}
    \end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-12 -->
<!-- proposition-13: body_md -->
\label{prop:projected-z-feedback-main-comp}
    For every $t \le T$, we have
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:Ifbmain-comp}
        \left\lvert \mathbb{E}\left[\mathrm{I}^{(\mathrm{fb},\mathrm{main})}_t(f_i) - \mathrm{I}^{(\mathrm{fb},\mathrm{main})}_t(\widetilde f_i) \right]\right\rvert \le Cd^{-5/2}
    \end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-13 -->
<!-- proposition-14: body_md -->
\label{prop:projected-z-complete}
    For any $t \le T$ and any $\lambda_i \in \mathbb R^p$ that is $\mathcal{G}_i$-measurable and satisfies $\left\lVert \lambda_i\right\rVert \le C d^{-1/2}$ a.s., we have
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:projected-z-proved}
        \left\lvert \mathbb{E} \left\langle  \lambda_i,z^t_i(\widetilde f_i)\right\rangle - \mathbb{E}\left\langle \lambda_i,z^t_i(f_i)\right\rangle\right\rvert \le C d^{-5/2}
    \end{equation}
    That is, \eqref{eq:projected-z-needed} of Proposition \ref{prop:remaining-linear} holds
<!-- end proposition-14 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
By \eqref{eq:projected-z-I234}, with $\mathrm{I}^{(2)}_t$, $\mathrm{I}^{(3)}_t$, and $\mathrm{I}^{(4)}_t$ defined in \eqref{eq:I234-def}, we have
    \begin{equation}
        \left\langle \lambda_i,z^t_i(u)\right\rangle = \mathrm{I}^{(2)}_t(u) + \mathrm{I}^{(3)}_t(u) + \mathrm{I}^{(4)}_t(u)
    \end{equation}
    Proposition \ref{prop:projected-z-cubic-quartic} shows that the cubic and quartic pieces contribute $\mathcal{O}(d^{-5/2})$. Proposition \ref{prop:projected-z-quad-reduction} reduces the quadratic term to $\mathrm{I}^{(2,\mathrm{lin})}_t$ and $\mathrm{I}^{(\mathrm{fb},\mathrm{main})}_t$, and Proposition \ref{prop:projected-z-quad-lin-comp} and Proposition \ref{prop:projected-z-feedback-main-comp} bound the differences of these between GOE and rank-one models in $\mathcal{O}(d^{-5/2})$. Therefore, we get \eqref{eq:projected-z-proved}
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
This follows from Propositions~\ref{prop:remaining-linear} and \ref{prop:projected-z-complete}.
<!-- end proof-18 -->
<!-- proof-19: body_md -->
Using \eqref{eq:bar-q-linear-combo}, with $\mathcal B_{t,r}$ defined in \eqref{eq:Btr-def}, we have
    \begin{equation}
        \bar q^t(u) = \sum_{r=0}^{t-1} \mathcal B_{t,r}u \ \beta^r_{i,0}(u)
    \end{equation}
    where each $\mathcal B_{t,r}$ is $\mathcal{G}_i$-measurable. We thus have
    \begin{equation}
        \left\langle \lambda_i,\bar q^t(u)\right\rangle = \sum_{r=0}^{t-1} \left\langle \mathcal B_{t,r}^\top \lambda_i,u\right\rangle \beta^r_{i,0}(u)
    \end{equation}
    With $\beta^r_{i,0}$ as defined in \eqref{eq:beta0-def}, write
    \begin{equation}
        a^r_i \,\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\, {\mathbf s}^* - \widehat{\mathbf s}^r_{\setminus i} \,\in\, \mathbb R^p, \quad \beta^{r}_{i,0} \,=\, \kappa_r \left\langle u,a^r_i\right\rangle +\nu_r\epsilon_i
    \end{equation}
    Conditioning on $\mathcal{G}_i$, using Lemma \ref{lem:one-column-moments}, we have
    \begin{equation}
        \mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle \mathcal B_{t,r}^\top\lambda_i,u\right\rangle\beta^r_{i,0}(u) \ \big| \ \mathcal{G}_i \right] = \frac{\kappa_r}{p} \left\langle \mathcal B_{t,r}^\top\lambda_i,a^r_i\right\rangle
    \end{equation}
    because the covariance matches as $I_p / p$ by \eqref{eq:column-covariance} and the term involving $\nu_r \epsilon_i$ vanishes as $u$ is centered. Notice that the RHS does not depend on $u$, so the conditional expectation is the same for $f_i, \widetilde f_i$. Summing over $r$ proves the claim.
<!-- end proof-19 -->
<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
By \eqref{eq:q-minus-barq-decomp}, with $\mathcal P_{k,t}$, $d^k(u)$, and $z_i^t(u)$ as in \eqref{eq:response-operator-def}, \eqref{eq:d-def} of Proposition \ref{prop:bar-alpha}, and \eqref{eq:z-unrolled}, we have
    \begin{equation}
        q^t(u) - \bar q^t(u) = \sum_{k=0}^{t-1} \mathcal P_{k,t}u \ d^k(u) + z^t_i(u)
    \end{equation}
    \begin{equation}
        \implies\, \left\langle  \lambda_i,q^t(u) - \bar q^t(u)\right\rangle \,=\, \sum_{k\,=\,0}^{t - 1} \left\langle \mathcal P_{k,t}^\top \lambda_i,u\right\rangle d^k(u) + \left\langle \lambda_i,z^t_i(u)\right\rangle
    \end{equation}
    Since we have $\left\lVert \lambda_i\right\rVert \le C d^{-1/2}$, use Lemma \ref{lem:response-op-bounds} and Cauchy--Schwarz, we have
    \begin{equation}
        \left\lVert \mathcal P_{k,t}^\top \lambda_i\right\rVert_{L^{2m}} \le C_m d^{-1/2}
    \end{equation}
    From Corollary \ref{cor:one-column-moments}, we have
    \begin{equation}
       \left\lVert \left\langle \mathcal P^\top_{k,t}\lambda_i,u\right\rangle\right\rVert_{L^{2m}} \le C_m d^{-3/2}
    \end{equation}
    So together with Proposition \ref{prop:bar-alpha}, we have
    \begin{equation}
        \mathbb{E}\left\lvert \left\langle \mathcal P_{k,t}^\top \lambda_i,u\right\rangle d^k(u)\right\rvert \,\le\, \left\lVert \left\langle \mathcal P^\top_{k,t} \lambda_i,u\right\rangle\right\rVert_{L^2} \left\lVert d^k(u)\right\rVert_{L^2} \,\le\, C d^{-5/2}
    \end{equation}
    Since $t \le T$, summing over $k$ and using Proposition \ref{prop:leading-linear-barq}, we obtain \eqref{eq:linear-remainder-reduction}. If \eqref{eq:projected-z-needed} holds, then \eqref{eq:remaining-linear-conclusion} follows immediately from \eqref{eq:linear-remainder-reduction}.
<!-- end proof-20 -->
<!-- proof-21: body_md -->
Iterating the block recursion \eqref{eq:block-recursion}, with $\beta_i^k(u)$ and $e_i^k(u)$ defined in \eqref{eq:beta-exact} of Proposition \ref{prop:exact-loo} and \eqref{eq:block-error-def}, gives
    \begin{equation}
        \delta^t(u) \,=\, \sum_{k\,=\,0}^{t-1} M_i^{t-1} \cdots M_i^{k + 1} B^k_i u \beta^k_i(u) + \sum_{\ell\,=\,0}^{t-1} M_i^{t-1} \cdots M_i^{\ell + 1}e^\ell_i(u).
    \end{equation}
    Applying $\Gamma_t$ and adding $a_tu\beta_i^t(u)$ gives \eqref{eq:m-exact-unrolled}. The moment bounds for $K_i$ and $M_i^r$ in Lemmas~\ref{lem:sensing-envelope} and \ref{lem:response-op-bounds} give \eqref{eq:m-response-op-bound}.

    Since $e^\ell_i(u) = \Pi_1^\top \varepsilon^\ell_i(u)$ and each $\varepsilon^\ell_i(u) = \mathcal{O}_{L^m}(d^{-1})$ by Proposition \ref{prop:delta-moments}, the same response-operator bound gives \eqref{eq:zeta-exact-bound}. Now we plug the expansion of $\bar \alpha^t(u)$ from \eqref{eq:bar-alpha-linear-combo} of Proposition \ref{prop:bar-alpha} into \eqref{eq:mbar-def}, with $\mathcal M_{k,t}$ defined in \eqref{eq:m-response-def}, to get
    \begin{equation}
        \begin{aligned}
            \bar m^t(u) \,=\, \sum_{k\,=\,0}^t \sum_{r\,=\,0}^k \mathcal M_{k,t} u c_{k,r} \beta^r_{i,0}(u) \,=\, \sum_{r \,=\, 0}^t \left( \sum_{k\,=\,r}^t c_{k,r} \mathcal M_{k,t} \right) u \beta^r_{i,0}(u).
        \end{aligned}
    \end{equation}
    Therefore, \eqref{eq:mbar-linear-combo} holds with
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:Rtr-def}
        \mathcal R_{t,r} \mathrel{\mathop{:}}= \sum_{k=r}^t c_{k,r} \mathcal M_{k,t},
    \end{equation}
    which is $\mathcal{G}_i$-measurable. By subtracting from \eqref{eq:m-exact-unrolled}, we have
    \begin{equation}
        m^t(u) - \bar m^t(u) \,=\, \sum_{k\,=\,0}^t \mathcal M_{k,t} u \left(\beta^k_i(u) - \bar \alpha^k(u) \right) + \zeta^t(u) \,=\, \zeta^t(u) + s^t(u)
    \end{equation}
    which gives \eqref{eq:m-minus-mbar-decomp}. From \eqref{eq:mbar-linear-combo}, Corollary \ref{cor:scalar-bounds}, Lemma \ref{lem:sensing-envelope}, and \eqref{eq:m-response-op-bound}, we get \eqref{eq:mbar-size}. Lastly, \eqref{eq:s-remainder-bound} follows from the definition of $s^t$ in \eqref{eq:s-remainder-def}, Proposition \ref{prop:bar-alpha}, Lemma \ref{lem:sensing-envelope}, and \eqref{eq:m-response-op-bound}.
<!-- end proof-21 -->
<!-- proof-22: body_md -->
From \eqref{eq:vector-local-bound} and \eqref{eq:mbar-size} of Lemma \ref{lem:m-representation}, we immediately have \eqref{eq:epsilon-quad-lin-size}. For \eqref{eq:epsilon-quad-lin-error}, we write
    \begin{equation}
        m^k(u) = \bar m^k(u) + r^k(u), \quad r^k(u) \mathrel{\mathop{:}}= s^k(u) + \zeta^k(u)
    \end{equation}
    Then \eqref{eq:m-minus-mbar-decomp} of Lemma \ref{lem:m-representation}, \eqref{eq:s-remainder-bound} of Lemma \ref{lem:m-representation}, and \eqref{eq:zeta-exact-bound} of Lemma \ref{lem:m-representation} give
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:r-m-size}
        \left\lVert r^k(u)\right\rVert_{L^m} \le C_m d^{-1}
    \end{equation}
    By \eqref{eq:epsilon-third-order-vector}, with $\mathbf B^k,\mathbf T^k$ and $\rho_{i,k}^{(4)}$ defined in \eqref{eq:vector-local-def} and \eqref{eq:vector-local-remainder-def}, we have
    \begin{equation}
        \begin{aligned}
            \varepsilon^k_i(u) - \varepsilon^k_{i,\mathrm{quad},\mathrm{lin}}(u) &= 2 \mathbf B^k(\bar m^k(u), r^k(u)) + \mathbf B^k(r^k(u), r^k(u)) \\
            \ &+ \mathbf T^k(m^k(u), m^k(u), m^k(u)) + \rho^{(4)}_{i,k}(m^k(u))
        \end{aligned}
    \end{equation}
    Now apply \eqref{eq:vector-local-bound}, \eqref{eq:mbar-size} of Lemma \ref{lem:m-representation}, \eqref{eq:r-m-size}, and Proposition \ref{prop:delta-moments}, we have
    \begin{equation}
        2\left\lVert \mathbf B^k(\bar m^k(u), r^k(u))\right\rVert_{L^m} \,\le\, C_m d^{-3/2}, \quad \left\lVert \mathbf B^k(r^k(u), r^k(u))\right\rVert_{L^m} \,\le\, C_m d^{-2}
    \end{equation}
    \begin{equation}
        \left\lVert \mathbf T^k(m^k(u), m^k(u), m^k(u))\right\rVert_{L^m} \,\le\, C_m d^{-3/2},\quad \left\lVert \rho^{(4)}_{i,k}(m^k)\right\rVert_{L^m} \,\le\, C_m d^{-2}
    \end{equation}
    So the conclusion follows.
<!-- end proof-22 -->
<!-- proof-23: body_md -->
With $\vartheta_{k,t}$ defined in \eqref{eq:vartheta-def}, for the quartic term we have from Proposition \ref{prop:delta-moments}, \eqref{eq:vector-local-bound}, and \eqref{eq:vartheta-bound} of Lemma \ref{lem:projected-z-representation} that
    \begin{equation}
        \mathbb{E}\left\lvert \mathrm{I}^{(4)}_t(u)\right\rvert \,\le\, \sum_{k\,=\,0}^{t-1} \left\lVert \vartheta_{k,t}\right\rVert_{L^2} \left\lVert \rho^{(4)}_{i,k}(m^k(u))\right\rVert_{L^{2}} \,\le\, C d^{-1/2}d^{-2} \,\le\, Cd^{-5/2}
    \end{equation}
    uniformly in $u \in \{f_i, \widetilde f_i\}$. For the cubic term, we write
    \begin{equation}
        m^k(u) = \bar m^k(u) + r^k(u), \quad r^k(u) = s^k(u) + \zeta^k(u)
    \end{equation}
    so by \eqref{eq:zeta-exact-bound} of Lemma \ref{lem:m-representation}, \eqref{eq:mbar-size} of Lemma \ref{lem:m-representation}, and \eqref{eq:s-remainder-bound} of Lemma \ref{lem:m-representation}, we have
    \begin{equation}
        \left\lVert \bar m^k(u)\right\rVert_{L^m} \le C_m d^{-1/2}, \quad \left\lVert r^k(u)\right\rVert_{L^m} \le C_m d^{-1}
    \end{equation}
    Now, with $\mathfrak T_{k,t}$ defined in \eqref{eq:projected-BT-def}, we expand
    \begin{equation}
        \begin{aligned}
            &\mathfrak T_{k,t}(m^k(u), m^k(u), m^k(u)) - \mathfrak T_{k,t}(\bar m^k(u), \bar m^k(u), \bar m^k(u)) \\
            =& \mathfrak T_{k,t}(r^k, \bar m^k, \bar m^k) + \mathfrak T_{k,t}(\bar m^k, r^k, \bar m^k) + \mathfrak T_{k,t}(\bar m^k, \bar m^k, r^k) + \mathfrak T_{k,t}(r^k,r^k,\bar m^k) \\
            +& \mathfrak T_{k,t}(r^k, \bar m^k, r^k) + \mathfrak T_{k,t}(\bar m^k, r^k, r^k) + \mathfrak T_{k,t}(r^k,r^k, r^k)
        \end{aligned}
    \end{equation}
    where we have suppressed the argument $u$. Each term contains at least one factor of $r^k(u)$. Applying \eqref{eq:projected-local-T-moment} and \eqref{eq:vartheta-bound} gives
    \begin{equation}
        \begin{aligned}
            &\left\lVert \mathfrak T_{k,t}(m^k(u), m^k(u), m^k(u)) - \mathfrak T_{k,t}(\bar m^k(u), \bar m^k(u), \bar m^k(u))\right\rVert_{L^1} \\
            &\le\, C d^{-1/2}\left( \left\lVert \bar m^k(u)\right\rVert_{L^4}^2 \left\lVert r^k(u)\right\rVert_{L^4} + \left\lVert \bar m^k(u)\right\rVert_{L^4} \left\lVert r^k(u)\right\rVert_{L^4}^2 + \left\lVert r^k(u)\right\rVert_{L^4}^3 \right)  \,\le\, Cd^{-5/2}
        \end{aligned}
    \end{equation}
    Therefore, it remains to bound $\mathfrak T_{k,t}(\bar m^k, \bar m^k, \bar m^k)$. By \eqref{eq:mbar-linear-combo} of Lemma \ref{lem:m-representation}, with $\mathcal R_{k,r}$ defined in \eqref{eq:Rtr-def}, we have $\bar m^k(u) = \sum_{r=0}^k \mathcal R_{k,r} u \beta^r_{i,0}(u)$. So we can write
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:cubic-main-expand}
        \mathfrak T_{k,t} (\bar m^k, \bar m^k, \bar m^k) \,=\, \sum_{r,s,q \,=\, 0}^k \beta^r_{i,0}(u) \beta^s_{i,0}(u) \beta^q_{i,0}(u) \mathfrak C_{k,t,r,s,q}(u,u,u)
    \end{equation}
    where the trilinear form $\mathfrak C$ is defined by
    \begin{equation}
        \mathfrak C_{k,t,r,s,q}(u_1, u_2, u_3) \mathrel{\mathop{:}}= \mathfrak T_{k,t}(\mathcal R_{k,r} u_1, \mathcal R_{k,s} u_2, \mathcal R_{k,q} u_3)
    \end{equation}
    Since $k\le T$ is fixed, the sum contains only a fixed number of terms, so it suffices to bound each summand separately. We will decompose $\mathfrak C_{k,t,r,s,q}(u,u,u)$ into a linear term and a centered quadratic-form term, and then combine their $L^2$ bounds with $\beta^r_{i,0}(u)\beta^s_{i,0}(u)\beta^q_{i,0}(u)=\mathcal{O}_{L^2}(d^{-3/2})$ using Cauchy--Schwarz. Using \eqref{eq:mbar-linear-combo} of Lemma \ref{lem:m-representation}, \eqref{eq:projected-local-HS-bound}, and Lemma \ref{lem:hs-composition}, we have
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:cubic-main-HS}
        \left\lVert \mathfrak C_{k,t;r,s,q}\right\rVert_{L^m(\mathrm{HS})}\le C_m d^{1/2}.
    \end{equation}
    We apply Lemma \ref{lem:trilinear-decomp} to get
    \begin{equation}
        \mathfrak C_{k,t,r,s,q}(u,u,u) \,=\, \left\langle b,u\right\rangle + \sum_{\nu\,=\,1}^p \left\langle e_\nu,u\right\rangle \left( u^\top M_\nu u - \frac 1 p \mathrm{Tr}(M_\nu) \right)
    \end{equation}
    From \eqref{eq:cubic-main-HS}, Lemma \ref{lem:trilinear-decomp} also gets
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:b-M-sum-bound}
        \left\lVert b\right\rVert_{L^m} \,\le\, C_m d^{-1/2}, \quad \left\lVert \sum_{\nu\,=\,1}^p \left\lVert M_\nu\right\rVert_\mathrm{F}^2\right\rVert_{L^m} \,\le\, C_m d
    \end{equation}
    For the $\left\langle b,u\right\rangle$ term, we have from Corollary \ref{cor:one-column-moments}(i),
    \begin{equation}
        \left\lVert \left\langle b,u\right\rangle\right\rVert_{L^2} \le Cd^{-3/2}
    \end{equation}
    while Corollary \ref{cor:scalar-bounds} implies
    \begin{equation}
        \left\lVert \beta^r_{i,0}(u) \beta^s_{i,0}(u) \beta^q_{i,0}(u)\right\rVert_{L^2} \le C d^{-3/2}
    \end{equation}
    Therefore, Cauchy--Schwarz implies
    \begin{equation}
        \mathbb{E} \left\lvert \beta^r_{i,0}(u)\beta^s_{i,0}(u) \beta^q_{i,0}(u) \left\langle b,u\right\rangle \right\rvert \le Cd^{-3}
    \end{equation}
    which is $o(d^{-5/2})$ and therefore $\mathcal{O}(d^{-5/2})$. For the $\nu$-sum, define
    \begin{equation}
        Y(u) \mathrel{\mathop{:}}= \sum_{\nu=1}^p \left\langle e_\nu,u\right\rangle \left(u^\top M_\nu u - \frac 1 p \mathrm{Tr}(M_\nu) \right)
    \end{equation}
    By Cauchy--Schwarz,  
    \begin{equation}
        \begin{aligned}
            \left\lvert Y(u)\right\rvert &= \left\lvert \sum_{\nu = 1}^p \left\langle e_\nu,u\right\rangle \left( u^\top M_\nu u - \frac 1 p \mathrm{Tr}(M_\nu) \right)\right\rvert \\
            &\le\, \left( \sum_{\nu \,=\, 1}^p \left\langle e_\nu,u\right\rangle^2 \right)^{1/2} \left( \sum_{\nu \,=\, 1}^p \left| u^\top M_\nu u - \frac 1 p \mathrm{Tr}(M_\nu) \right|^2 \right)^{1/2} \\
            &= \left\lVert u\right\rVert \left( \sum_{\nu = 1}^p \left\lvert u^\top M_\nu u - \frac 1 p \mathrm{Tr}(M_\nu)\right\rvert^2 \right)^{1/2}
        \end{aligned}
    \end{equation}
    Write the conditional moment norm as
    \begin{equation}
        \left\lVert X\right\rVert_{L^r(u | \mathcal{G}_i)} \mathrel{\mathop{:}}= \left( \mathbb{E}[|X|^r \ | \ \mathcal{G}_i] \right)^{1/r}
    \end{equation}
    By conditional H\"older, we have
    \begin{equation}
        \left\lVert Y(u)\right\rVert_{L^2(u | \mathcal{G}_i)} \,\le\, \left\lVert u\right\rVert_{L^4(u | \mathcal{G}_i)} \left\lVert \left(  \sum_{\nu \,=\, 1}^p \left\lvert u^\top M_\nu u - \frac 1 p \mathrm{Tr}(M_\nu\right\rvert^2 \right)^{1/2}\right\rVert_{L^4(u | \mathcal{G}_i)}
    \end{equation}
    Since $u$ is independent from $\mathcal{G}_i$, we have from \ref{lem:sensing-envelope} a.s., $\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{L^4(u | \mathcal{G}_i)} = \left\lVert u\right\rVert_{L^4} \le C$. For the second factor, we have by Minkowski,
    \begin{equation}
        \begin{aligned}
            &\left\lVert \left( \sum_{\nu \,=\, 1}^p \left\lvert u^\top M_\nu u - \frac 1 p \mathrm{Tr}(M_\nu)\right\rvert^2 \right)^{1/2}\right\rVert_{L^4(u | \mathcal{G}_i)}^2 \,=\, \left\lVert \sum_{\nu \,=\, 1}^p \left\lvert u^\top M_\nu u - \frac 1 p \mathrm{Tr}(M_\nu)\right\rvert^2\right\rVert_{L^2(u | \mathcal{G}_i)} \\
            \le\, &\sum_{\nu \,=\, 1}^p \left\lVert \left(u^\top M_\nu u - \frac 1 p \mathrm{Tr}(M_\nu)\right)^2\right\rVert_{L^2(u | \mathcal{G}_i)} \,=\, \sum_{i \,=\, 1}^p \left\lVert u^\top M_\nu u - \frac 1 p \mathrm{Tr}(M_\nu)\right\rVert_{L^4(u | \mathcal{G}_i)}^2
        \end{aligned}
    \end{equation}
    Lemma \ref{lem:refined-centered-quad} implies
    \begin{equation}
        \left\lVert u^\top M_\nu u - \frac 1 p \mathrm{Tr}(M_\nu)\right\rVert_{L^4(u | \mathcal{G}_i)} \le C d^{-3/2} \left\lVert M_\nu\right\rVert_\mathrm{F}
    \end{equation}
    Therefore, almost surely, we have
    \begin{equation}
        \begin{aligned}
            \left\lVert Y(u)\right\rVert_{L^2(u | \mathcal{G}_i)} &\le\, C\left( \sum_{\nu \,=\, 1}^p \left\lVert u^\top M_\nu u - \frac 1 p \mathrm{Tr}(M_\nu)\right\rVert_{L^4(u | \mathcal{G}_i)}^2 \right)^{1/2} \,\le\, C d^{-3/2} \left( \sum_{\nu \,=\, 1}^p \left\lVert M_\nu\right\rVert_\mathrm{F}^2 \right)^{1/2}
        \end{aligned}
    \end{equation}
    Taking expectations over $\mathcal{G}_i$ gives
    \begin{equation}
        \begin{aligned}
            \left\lVert Y(u)\right\rVert_{L^2} &=\, \left\lVert \left\lVert Y(u)\right\rVert_{L^2(u | \mathcal{G}_i)}\right\rVert_{L^2} \,\le\, C d^{-3/2} \left\lVert \left( \sum_{\nu \,=\, 1}^p \left\lVert M_\nu\right\rVert_\mathrm{F}^2 \right)^{1/2}\right\rVert_{L^2} \,=\, Cd^{-3/2} \left\lVert \sum_{\nu \,=\, 1}^p \left\lVert M_\nu\right\rVert_\mathrm{F}^2\right\rVert_{L^1}^{1/2}
        \end{aligned}
    \end{equation}
    Using \eqref{eq:b-M-sum-bound} with $m = 1$ gives
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:Yu-L2-bound}
        \left\lVert Y(u)\right\rVert_{L^2} \le C \cdot d^{-3/2} \cdot d^{1/2} \le Cd^{-1}
    \end{equation}
    Now since $\beta^r_{i,0}\beta^s_{i,0}\beta^q_{i,0} = \mathcal{O}_{L^2}(d^{-3/2})$, we get
    \begin{equation}
        \mathbb{E}\left\lvert \beta^r_{i,0}(u) \beta^s_{i,0}(u) \beta^q_{i,0}(u) Y(u)\right\rvert \le C d^{-5/2}
    \end{equation}
    Since the sum in \eqref{eq:cubic-main-expand} is over a fixed number of terms, we have $\mathcal{O}(d^{-5/2})$ contribution, which proves \eqref{eq:I34-comparison}.
<!-- end proof-23 -->
<!-- proof-24: body_md -->
The sums in \eqref{eq:I234-def} and \eqref{eq:quadratic-main-terms} contain a fixed number of terms, so we bound each term separately. Fix $k<t$ and abbreviate
    $$\bar m \mathrel{\mathop{:}}= \bar m^k(u),\quad s = s^k(u),\quad z = \zeta^k_{\mathrm{quad},\mathrm{lin}}(u), \quad \tilde z = \tilde \zeta^k(u)
    $$
    Then we have $m^k(u) = \bar m + s + z + \tilde z$ by \eqref{eq:m-minus-mbar-decomp} of Lemma \ref{lem:m-representation} and \eqref{eq:zeta-quad-lin-def}. Expanding the bilinear form gives us
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:quad-reduction-expand}
            \begin{aligned}
            \mathfrak B_{k,t}(m^k(u),m^k(u))
            &=\mathfrak B_{k,t}(\bar m,\bar m)
            +2\mathfrak B_{k,t}(\bar m,z)
            +2\mathfrak B_{k,t}(\bar m,s)
            +2\mathfrak B_{k,t}(\bar m,\tilde z)\\
            &+\mathfrak B_{k,t}(s,s)
            +2\mathfrak B_{k,t}(s,z)
            +2\mathfrak B_{k,t}(s,\tilde z)\\
            &+\mathfrak B_{k,t}(z,z)
            +2\mathfrak B_{k,t}(z,\tilde z)
            +\mathfrak B_{k,t}(\tilde z,\tilde z).
        \end{aligned}
    \end{equation}
    We will show that only the first two terms on the RHS matter. By \eqref{eq:mbar-size} of Lemma \ref{lem:m-representation}, \eqref{eq:s-remainder-bound} of Lemma \ref{lem:m-representation}, \eqref{eq:zeta-quad-lin-size}, and \eqref{eq:zeta-quad-lin-error}, we have
    \begin{equation}
        \left\lVert \bar m\right\rVert_{L^m} \,\le\, C_m d^{-1/2}, \quad \left\lVert s\right\rVert_{L^m} + \left\lVert z\right\rVert_{L^m} \,\le\, C_m d^{-1}, \quad \left\lVert \tilde z\right\rVert_{L^m} \,\le\, C_m d^{-3/2}
    \end{equation}
    Observe that every term in \eqref{eq:quad-reduction-expand} that contains any two factors among $\{s, z, \tilde z\}$, possibly repeated, has $L^1$-norm bounded by $\mathcal{O}(d^{-5/2})$ by \eqref{eq:projected-local-B-moment}. Terms with $\bar m$ mixed with $\tilde z$ also satisfy $\mathcal{O}(d^{-5/2})$ bound. In particular these terms are bounded by H\"older
    \begin{equation}
        C d^{-1/2}\times
        \begin{cases}
            \left\lVert \bar m\right\rVert_{L^3}\left\lVert \tilde z\right\rVert_{L^3},\\
            \left\lVert s\right\rVert_{L^3}\left\lVert s\right\rVert_{L^3},\\
            \left\lVert s\right\rVert_{L^3}\left\lVert z\right\rVert_{L^3},\\
            \left\lVert z\right\rVert_{L^3}\left\lVert z\right\rVert_{L^3},
        \end{cases} = \mathcal{O}(d^{-5/2})
    \end{equation}
    Therefore, it remains only to control $\mathfrak B_{k,t}(\bar m,s)$. Recall the formula for $\bar m^t, s^t$ from \eqref{eq:mbar-linear-combo} of Lemma \ref{lem:m-representation} and \eqref{eq:s-remainder-def} of Lemma \ref{lem:m-representation}, with $\beta^r_{i,0}(u)$, $d^a(u)$, and $\mathcal M_{a,k}$ defined in \eqref{eq:beta0-def}, \eqref{eq:d-def} of Proposition \ref{prop:bar-alpha}, and \eqref{eq:m-response-def}; we have
    \begin{equation}
        \mathfrak B_{k,t}(\bar m, s) = \sum_{r,a=0}^k \beta^r_{i,0}(u) d^a(u) \mathfrak{Q}_{k,t,r,a}(u,u)
    \end{equation}
    where
    \begin{equation}
        \mathfrak{Q}_{k,t,r,a}(u_1, u_2) \mathrel{\mathop{:}}= \mathfrak B_{k,t}(\mathcal R_{k,r}u_1, \mathcal M_{a,k}u_2)
    \end{equation}
