        +\sum_{j>i}\left\lVert f_j\right\rVert^2_{L^{2m}}
        \le C_m n
        \le C_m d^2.
    \end{equation}
    Let $E_i=\{\left\lVert K_i\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\le C\}$. Then by Cauchy--Schwarz
    \begin{equation}
        \mathbb{E}\left\lVert K_i\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}^m
        \le C^m+\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{Tr}(K_i)^m\mathbb I_{E_i^c}\big]
        \le C^m+\big\|\mathrm{Tr}(K_i)\big\|_{L^{2m}}^m\mathbb P(E_i^c)^{1/2}
        \le C_m,
    \end{equation}
    for all sufficiently large $p$. So
    \begin{equation}
    \left\lVert K_i\right\rVert_{L^m(\mathrm{op})}\le C_m.
    \end{equation}

    Conditioned on $F_{\setminus i}$, the vector $F_{\setminus i}\epsilon$ is Gaussian with covariance $K_i$. Let $(\lambda_1, \ldots, \lambda_r$ be the eigenvalues of $K_i$. Hence
    \begin{equation}
        \begin{aligned}
        \mathbb{E}\left[\left\lVert F_{\setminus i}\epsilon\right\rVert^{2m}\mid F_{\setminus i}\right]
        &= \mathbb{E}\left[ \left( \sum_{j = 1}^r \lambda_j g_j^2\right)^m\right], \qquad g_j \stackrel{i.i.d}{\sim} \mathcal{N}(0, 1) \\
        &=\, \sum_{\alpha_1 + \cdots + \alpha_r \,=\, m} \binom{m}{\alpha_1, \ldots, \alpha_r} \prod_{j \,=\, 1}^t \lambda_j^{\alpha_j} \mathbb{E}[g_j^{2\alpha_j}]
        \end{aligned}
    \end{equation}
    But $\prod_{j = 1}^r \mathbb{E}[g_j^{2\alpha_j}] \le C_m$. Therefore,
    \begin{equation}
         \mathbb{E}\left[\left\lVert F_{\setminus i}\epsilon\right\rVert^{2m}\mid F_{\setminus i}\right] \,\le\, C_m \sum_{\alpha_1+ \ldots+ \alpha_r \,=\, m} \binom{m}{\alpha_1, \ldots, \alpha_r} \prod_{j \,=\, 1}^r \lambda_j^{\alpha_j} \,=\, C_m \left( \sum_{j \,=\, 1}^r \lambda_j \right)^m \,=\, C_m \mathrm{Tr}(K_i)^m
    \end{equation}
    Using again
    \begin{equation}
        \mathrm{Tr}(K_i)\,=\,\sum_{j\,<\,i}\left\lVert \widetilde f_j\right\rVert^2+\sum_{j\,>\,i}\left\lVert f_j\right\rVert^2
    \end{equation}
    and the bound $n\leq C_0p=\mathcal{O}(d^2)$, we obtain $\|\mathrm{Tr}(K_i)\|_{L^m}\le C_m d^2$. Taking expectations gives
    \begin{equation}
        \left\lVert F_{\setminus i}\epsilon\right\rVert_{L^m}\le C_m d.
    \end{equation}
    This completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-41 -->
<!-- proof-42: body_md -->
Since $u$ is independent of $\mathcal G_i$, it is enough to prove the deterministic version.
    \noindent \emph{Gaussian column.}
    If $u=\widetilde f_i$, then $u\sim N(0,I_p/p)$. The identities \eqref{eq:linear-moment-1}--\eqref{eq:linear-moment-2} are immediate. The bound \eqref{eq:linear-moment-lm} follows from the fact that $\left\langle u,a\right\rangle$ is centered Gaussian with variance $p^{-1}\left\lVert a\right\rVert^2$. For \eqref{eq:quadratic-moment-lm}, only the symmetric part of $A$ matters:
    \begin{equation}
        \left\langle u,Au\right\rangle=\left\langle u,A_{\mathrm s}u\right\rangle
    \end{equation}
    where $A_s = \frac{A + A^\top}{2}$.

    The centered quadratic form
    \begin{equation}
        \left\langle u,A_{\mathrm s}u\right\rangle-\frac1p\mathrm{Tr} A_{\mathrm s}
    \end{equation}
    has variance
    \begin{equation}
        \text{Var}\left(\left\langle u,A_{\mathrm s}u\right\rangle\right)
        =\frac{2}{p^2}\mathrm{Tr}(A_{\mathrm s}^2)
        \le \frac{2}{p}\left\lVert A_{\mathrm s}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}^2
        \le C d^{-2}\left\lVert A_{\mathrm s}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}^2
    \end{equation}
    Gaussian hypercontractivity gives
    \begin{equation}
        \left\|\left\langle u,Au\right\rangle-\frac1p\mathrm{Tr} A\right\|_{L^m}\le C_m d^{-1}\left\lVert A_{\mathrm s}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}},
    \end{equation}
    which is even better than \eqref{eq:quadratic-moment-lm}.
    \noindent \emph{Rank-one column.}
    Now let $u=f_i$. Write
    \begin{equation}
        Y\mathrel{\mathop{:}}= \frac{x_ix_i^\top-I_d}{\sqrt{d(d+1)}}\in\textsf{Sym}_d,
        \qquad
        u=\operatorname{vec}(Y)
        \qquad
        x_i\sim N(0,I_d)
    \end{equation}
    Let $M=\mathrm{mat}(a)\in\textsf{Sym}_d$. Then
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:linear-form-r1}
        \left\langle u,a\right\rangle
        =\mathrm{Tr}(YM)
        =\frac{x_i^\top Mx_i-\mathrm{Tr} M}{\sqrt{d(d+1)}}.
    \end{equation}
    This is a centered Gaussian quadratic form, so \eqref{eq:linear-moment-1} holds. Also,
    \begin{equation}
        \mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle u,a\right\rangle\left\langle u,b\right\rangle\right]
        =\frac{2}{d(d+1)}\mathrm{Tr}(\mathrm{mat}(a)\mathrm{mat}(b))
        =\frac1p\left\langle a,b\right\rangle,
    \end{equation}
    which is \eqref{eq:linear-moment-2}. Since
    \begin{equation}
        \text{Var}(x_i^\top Mx_i-\mathrm{Tr} M)=2\left\lVert M\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}^2=2\left\lVert a\right\rVert^2,
    \end{equation}
    hypercontractivity for Gaussian quadratic forms gives \eqref{eq:linear-moment-lm}.

    It remains to prove \eqref{eq:quadratic-moment-lm}. Let $\mathscr{A}:\textsf{Sym}_d\to\textsf{Sym}_d$ be the self-adjoint operator
    \begin{equation}
        \mathscr{A}(H)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}= \mathrm{mat}(A_{\mathrm s}\operatorname{vec}(H)).
    \end{equation}
    Because $\operatorname{vec}$ is an isometry,
    \begin{equation}
        \left\lVert \mathscr{A}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}\to\mathrm{F}}=\left\lVert A_{\mathrm s}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}.
    \end{equation}
    Using $u=\operatorname{vec}(Y)$,
    \begin{equation}
        \left\langle u,Au\right\rangle=\left\langle Y,\mathscr{A} Y\right\rangle.
    \end{equation}
    By the covariance identity \eqref{eq:column-covariance},
    \begin{equation}
        \mathbb{E}\left\langle Y,\mathscr{A} Y\right\rangle
        =\frac1p\mathrm{Tr}(A_{\mathrm s})
        =\frac1p\mathrm{Tr} A.
    \end{equation}

    Write $Y_0=x_ix_i^\top-I_d$, so that $Y=Y_0/\sqrt{d(d+1)}$, and define
    \begin{equation}
        g(x_i)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}= \frac{1}{d(d+1)}\left\langle Y_0,\mathscr{A} Y_0\right\rangle.
    \end{equation}
    Then
    \begin{equation}
        g(x_i)=\left\langle u,Au\right\rangle.
    \end{equation}
    A direct differentiation gives, for every $h\in\mathbb R^d$,
    \begin{equation}
        Dg(x_i)[h]
        =\frac{4}{d(d+1)}\left\langle h,\mathscr{A}(Y_0)x_i\right\rangle,
    \end{equation}
    hence
    \begin{equation}
        \left\lVert \nabla g(x_i)\right\rVert
        \le \frac{4}{d(d+1)}\left\lVert \mathscr{A}\right\rVert_{F\to F}\left\lVert Y_0\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}\left\lVert x_i\right\rVert
        \le \frac{4}{d(d+1)}\left\lVert A_{\mathrm s}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\left\lVert Y_0\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}\left\lVert x_i\right\rVert.
    \end{equation}
    Let $S=\left\lVert x_i\right\rVert^2\sim \chi_d^2$. Since
    \begin{equation}
        \left\lVert Y_0\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}^2=S^2-2S+d,
    \end{equation}
    we obtain
    \begin{equation}
        \mathbb{E}\big[\left\lVert Y_0\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}^2\left\lVert x_i\right\rVert^2\big]
        =\mathbb{E}[S^3-2S^2+dS]
        =d^3+5d^2+4d
        \le C d^3.
    \end{equation}
    The Gaussian Poincar\'e inequality therefore gives
    \begin{equation}
        \text{Var}(g)\le \mathbb{E}\left\lVert \nabla g\right\rVert^2\le C d^{-1}\left\lVert A_{\mathrm s}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}^2.
    \end{equation}
    Since $g-\mathbb{E} g$ is a polynomial of degree at most four in $x_i$, Gaussian hypercontractivity yields
    \begin{equation}
        \left\|\left\langle u,Au\right\rangle-\frac1p\mathrm{Tr} A\right\|_{L^m}
        \le C_m \|g-\mathbb{E} g\|_{L^2}
        \le C_m d^{-1/2}\left\lVert A_{\mathrm s}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}.
    \end{equation}
    This proves \eqref{eq:quadratic-moment-lm}. The unconditional estimates \eqref{eq:linear-moment-uncond}--\eqref{eq:quadratic-moment-uncond} follow by taking expectations and using $p\asymp d^2$.
<!-- end proof-42 -->
<!-- proof-43: body_md -->
Fix $r \ge 2$, we write
    \begin{equation}
        A(t) \,\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\, \sum_{j \,=\, 1}^r t_j M_j, \quad t \,=\, (t_1, \ldots, t_r) \,\in\, \mathbb R^r
    \end{equation}
    Choose $t$ such that $\left\lVert A(t)\right\rVert_\mathrm{op} < 1/2$. We thus have
    \begin{equation}
        \mathbb{E} \exp\left( \sum_{j=1}^r t_j q_{M_j}(x) \right) = \exp\left(-\mathrm{Tr}(A(t))\right) \det(I_d - 2A(t))^{-1/2}
    \end{equation}
    The cumulant-generating function has the Taylor expansion
    \begin{equation}
        \begin{aligned}
            K(t) &\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\, \log \mathbb{E}\exp\left( \sum_{j\,=\,1}^r t_j q_{M_j}(x) \right) \,=\, -\mathrm{Tr} A(t) - \frac 1 2 \mathrm{Tr} \log(I_d - 2A(t)) \\
            &=\, -\mathrm{Tr} A(t) + \frac 1 2 \sum_{m\,=\,1}^\infty \frac{2^m}{m}\mathrm{Tr}(A(t)^m) \,=\, \sum_{m \,=\, 2}^\infty \frac{2^{m-1}}{m} \mathrm{Tr}(A(t)^m)
        \end{aligned}
    \end{equation}
    But the joint cumulants are given by the mixed derivative
    \begin{equation}
        \kappa\left(q_{M_1}(x), \ldots, q_{M_r}(x) \right) = \partial_{t_1} \cdots \partial_{t_r} K(t) \ \Big|_{t=0}
    \end{equation}
    But observe that $\frac{2^{m-1}}{m} \mathrm{Tr}(A(t)^m)$ is a polynomial of degree $m$ in $t$. Therefore, only the term with $m = r$ contributes to the $r$th joint cumulant. We thus have
    \begin{equation}
        \kappa\left(q_{M_1}(x), \ldots, q_{M_r}(x) \right) \,=\, \frac{2^{r-1}}{r} \sum_{\sigma \,\in\, S_r} \mathrm{Tr}\left( M_{\sigma(1)} \cdots M_{\sigma(r)} \right)
    \end{equation}
    Since trace is invariant under cyclic permutations, every cyclic class in $S_r$ contributes the same value to the sum and contains $r - 1$ permutations. Grouping the sum over $S_r$ into cyclic classes therefore gives the stated formula.
<!-- end proof-43 -->
<!-- proof-44: body_md -->
Since $u$ is independent of $\mathcal{G}_i$, it is enough to prove the deterministic version.

    \noindent \emph{Gaussian column.}
    If $u = \widetilde f_i$, then $u \sim \mathcal{N}(0, I_p/p)$. Note that
    \begin{equation}
        \left\langle u,Au\right\rangle - \frac 1 p \mathrm{Tr} A = \left\langle u,A_{\mathrm s}u\right\rangle - \frac 1 p \mathrm{Tr} A_{\mathrm s}.
    \end{equation}
    This is a centered Gaussian quadratic form with variance
    \begin{equation}
        \text{Var}(\left\langle u,A_{\mathrm s}u\right\rangle) \,=\, \frac{2}{p^2} \mathrm{Tr}(A_{\mathrm s}^2) \,=\, \frac{2}{p^2} \left\lVert A_{\mathrm s}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}^2.
    \end{equation}
    Gaussian hypercontractivity therefore gives
    \begin{equation}
        \left\lVert \left\langle u,Au\right\rangle - \frac 1 p \mathrm{Tr} A\right\rVert_{L^m} \,\le\, C_m p^{-1} \left\lVert A_{\mathrm s}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}} \,\le\, C_m d^{-2} \left\lVert A_{\mathrm s}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}},
    \end{equation}
    which is stronger than the claimed bound.

    \noindent \emph{Rank-one column.}
    Now let $u = f_i$. Write
    \begin{equation}
        Y \mathrel{\mathop{:}}= \frac{xx^\top - I_d}{\sqrt{d(d+1)}} \in \textsf{Sym}_d,
        \qquad
        u = \operatorname{vec}(Y),
        \qquad
        x \sim \mathcal{N}(0, I_d),
    \end{equation}
    and let $Y_0 = xx^\top - I_d$, so that $Y = Y_0 / \sqrt{d(d+1)}$. Recall the self-adjoint operator $\mathscr{A} : \textsf{Sym}_d \to \textsf{Sym}_d$ defined by
    \begin{equation}
        \mathscr{A}(H) \mathrel{\mathop{:}}= \mathrm{mat}(A_{\mathrm s} \operatorname{vec}(H)).
    \end{equation}
    Choose a Frobenius-orthonormal eigenbasis $(E_\alpha)_{\alpha = 1}^p$ of $\textsf{Sym}_d$ such that
    \begin{equation}
        \mathscr{A}(E_\alpha) = \lambda_\alpha E_\alpha,
        \qquad \alpha = 1, \ldots, p.
    \end{equation}
    Since $\operatorname{vec} \circ \mathscr{A} \circ \mathrm{mat} = A_{\mathrm s}$, the eigenvalues of $\mathscr{A}$ are the eigenvalues of $A_{\mathrm s}$. Therefore,
    \begin{equation}
        \sum_{\alpha = 1}^p \lambda_\alpha^2 = \left\lVert A_{\mathrm s}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}^2,
        \qquad
        \sum_{\alpha \,=\, 1}^p \lambda_\alpha \,=\, \mathrm{Tr} A_{\mathrm s} \,=\, \mathrm{Tr} A.
    \end{equation}

    For each $\alpha$, define
    \begin{equation}
        q_\alpha \mathrel{\mathop{:}}= \left\langle Y_0,E_\alpha\right\rangle = x^\top E_\alpha x - \mathrm{Tr}(E_\alpha).
    \end{equation}
    By Lemma \ref{lem:gaussian-quad-cumulants} with $r = 2$,
    \begin{equation}
        \mathbb{E}[q_\alpha q_\beta] = 2 \mathrm{Tr}(E_\alpha E_\beta) = 2 \delta_{\alpha\beta}.
    \end{equation}
    In particular, $\mathbb{E}[q_\alpha^2] = 2$ for every $\alpha$. Since $Y_0 = \sum_{\alpha = 1}^p q_\alpha E_\alpha$, we have
    \begin{equation}
        \left\langle u,Au\right\rangle \,=\, \left\langle Y,\mathscr{A} Y\right\rangle \,=\, \frac{1}{d(d+1)} \sum_{\alpha \,=\, 1}^p \lambda_\alpha q_\alpha^2.
    \end{equation}
    Using $p = d(d+1)/2$, we also get
    \begin{equation}
        \frac 1 p \mathrm{Tr} A \,=\, \frac{2}{d(d+1)} \sum_{\alpha \,=\, 1}^p \lambda_\alpha \,=\, \frac{1}{d(d+1)} \sum_{\alpha \,=\, 1}^p \lambda_\alpha \mathbb{E}[q_\alpha^2].
    \end{equation}
    Hence
    \begin{equation}
        \left\langle u,Au\right\rangle - \frac 1 p \mathrm{Tr} A = \frac{1}{d(d+1)} g(x),
        \qquad
        g(x) \mathrel{\mathop{:}}= \sum_{\alpha = 1}^p \lambda_\alpha (q_\alpha^2 - 2).
    \end{equation}
    The random variable $g(x)$ is a polynomial of degree four in the Gaussian vector $x$, so Gaussian hypercontractivity gives
    \begin{equation}
        \left\lVert g(x)\right\rVert_{L^m} \le C_m \left\lVert g(x)\right\rVert_{L^2}.
    \end{equation}

    It remains to bound $\text{Var}(g)$. Since each $q_\alpha$ is centered, the moment-cumulant formula gives
    \begin{equation}
        \text{Cov}(q_\alpha^2, q_\beta^2) = 2\kappa_2(q_\alpha, q_\beta)^2 + \kappa_4(q_\alpha, q_\alpha, q_\beta, q_\beta).
    \end{equation}
    By the previous display,
    \begin{equation}
        2\kappa_2(q_\alpha, q_\beta)^2 = 8\delta_{\alpha\beta}.
    \end{equation}
    By Lemma \ref{lem:gaussian-quad-cumulants} with $r = 4$, the fourth cumulant is a finite sum of traces of cyclic words of length four in $E_\alpha$ and $E_\beta$. Every such word is either of the form $E_\alpha^2 E_\beta^2$ or $E_\alpha E_\beta E_\alpha E_\beta$. Therefore,
    \begin{equation}
        \left|\kappa_4(q_\alpha, q_\alpha, q_\beta, q_\beta)\right|
        \le C \left(\mathrm{Tr}(E_\alpha^2 E_\beta^2) + \left|\mathrm{Tr}(E_\alpha E_\beta E_\alpha E_\beta)\right|\right).
    \end{equation}
    Since $E_\alpha$ and $E_\beta$ are symmetric, Cauchy--Schwarz gives
    \begin{equation}
        \left|\mathrm{Tr}(E_\alpha E_\beta E_\alpha E_\beta)\right|
        = \left|\mathrm{Tr}((E_\beta E_\alpha)(E_\alpha E_\beta))\right|
        \le \left\lVert E_\beta E_\alpha\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}} \left\lVert E_\alpha E_\beta\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}
        = \mathrm{Tr}(E_\alpha^2 E_\beta^2).
    \end{equation}
    Hence
    \begin{equation}
        \left|\text{Cov}(q_\alpha^2, q_\beta^2)\right| \le C \left(\delta_{\alpha\beta} + \mathrm{Tr}(E_\alpha^2 E_\beta^2)\right).
    \end{equation}
    Expanding the variance of $g$ gives
    \begin{equation}
        \text{Var}(g) \,\le\, C \sum_{\alpha \,=\, 1}^p \lambda_\alpha^2 + C \sum_{\alpha, \beta \,=\, 1}^p |\lambda_\alpha \lambda_\beta| \mathrm{Tr}(E_\alpha^2 E_\beta^2).
    \end{equation}

    To control the double sum, note first that $\sum_{\beta = 1}^p E_\beta^2$ does not depend on the choice of Frobenius-orthonormal basis of $\textsf{Sym}_d$. To see this, if $(\widetilde E_\beta)_{\beta = 1}^p$ is another such basis and $\widetilde E_\beta = \sum_{\gamma = 1}^p O_{\beta\gamma} E_\gamma$ for some $O \in O(p)$, then
    \begin{equation}
        \sum_{\beta \,=\, 1}^p \widetilde E_\beta^2 \,=\, \sum_{\beta, \gamma, \eta \,=\, 1}^p O_{\beta\gamma} O_{\beta\eta} E_\gamma E_\eta \,=\, \sum_{\gamma \,=\, 1}^p E_\gamma^2.
    \end{equation}
    Therefore we may compute this sum using the standard symmetric basis
    \begin{equation}
        F_{aa} = e_a e_a^\top,
        \qquad
        F_{ab} = \frac{e_a e_b^\top + e_b e_a^\top}{\sqrt 2},
        \qquad 1 \,\le\, a \,<\, b \,\le\, d.
    \end{equation}
    A direct calculation gives
    \begin{equation}
        \sum_{\beta = 1}^p E_\beta^2 = \frac{d+1}{2} I_d.
    \end{equation}

    Now set $T_{\alpha\beta} \mathrel{\mathop{:}}= \mathrm{Tr}(E_\alpha^2 E_\beta^2) \ge 0$. By Cauchy--Schwarz,
    \begin{equation}
        \begin{aligned}
            \sum_{\alpha, \beta = 1}^p |\lambda_\alpha \lambda_\beta| T_{\alpha\beta}
            &\le \left(\sum_{\alpha, \beta = 1}^p \lambda_\alpha^2 T_{\alpha\beta}\right)^{1/2}
            \left(\sum_{\alpha, \beta \,=\, 1}^p \lambda_\beta^2 T_{\alpha\beta}\right)^{1/2}\,=\, \sum_{\alpha, \beta \,=\, 1}^p \lambda_\alpha^2 T_{\alpha\beta} \\
            &=\, \sum_{\alpha \,=\, 1}^p \lambda_\alpha^2 \mathrm{Tr}\left(E_\alpha^2 \sum_{\beta \,=\, 1}^p E_\beta^2\right) \,=\, \frac{d+1}{2} \sum_{\alpha \,=\, 1}^p \lambda_\alpha^2.
        \end{aligned}
    \end{equation}
    We obtain
    \begin{equation}
        \text{Var}(g) \,\le\, C d \sum_{\alpha \,=\, 1}^p \lambda_\alpha^2 \,=\, C d \left\lVert A_{\mathrm s}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}^2.
    \end{equation}
    Therefore,
    \begin{equation}
        \left\lVert \left\langle u,Au\right\rangle - \frac 1 p \mathrm{Tr} A\right\rVert_{L^m}
        \le \frac{C_m}{d(d+1)} d^{1/2} \left\lVert A_{\mathrm s}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}
        \le C_m d^{-3/2} \left\lVert A_{\mathrm s}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}.
    \end{equation}

    This proves the deterministic estimate. Averaging over $\mathcal{G}_i$, we get
    \begin{equation}
        \left\lVert \left\langle u,Au\right\rangle - \frac 1 p \mathrm{Tr} A\right\rVert_{L^m}
        \le C_m d^{-3/2} \left\lVert A_{\mathrm s}\right\rVert_{L^m(\mathrm{F})}
        \le C_m d^{-3/2} \left\lVert A\right\rVert_{L^{2m}(\mathrm{F})},
    \end{equation}
    which proves the first claim. The second follows from $\left\lVert A_{\mathrm s}\right\rVert_{L^{2m}(\mathrm{F})} \le \left\lVert A\right\rVert_{L^{2m}(\mathrm{F})}$.
<!-- end proof-44 -->
<!-- proof-45: body_md -->
For $t = 0$, we clearly have $q^0(u) = 0$ because the iterates start at the same place. By \eqref{eq:w-exact} of Proposition \ref{prop:exact-loo}, we have
    \begin{equation}
        w^0(u) = u \left(\kappa_0 \left\langle u,{\mathbf s}^* - \widehat{\mathbf s}^0\right\rangle + \nu_0 \epsilon_i \right)
    \end{equation}
    But by Assumption \ref{ass:signal-init}, by triangle inequality,
    \begin{equation}
        \left\lVert {\mathbf s}^* - \widehat{\mathbf s}^0\right\rVert \leq C \sqrt{d}
    \end{equation}
    From \eqref{eq:linear-moment-uncond} of Lemma \ref{lem:one-column-moments} with $a = {\mathbf s}^* - \widehat{\mathbf s}^0$, we have
    \begin{equation}
        \left\lVert \left\langle u,{\mathbf s}^* - \widehat{\mathbf s}^0\right\rangle\right\rVert_{L^{2m}} \,\leq\, C_m d^{-1} \left\lVert {\mathbf s}^* - \widehat{\mathbf s}^0\right\rVert_{L^{2m}} \,\leq\, C_m d^{-1/2}
    \end{equation}
    Since we also have $\mathbb{E} \left\lVert u\right\rVert^m \leq C_m$ and $\nu_0 \epsilon_i = \mathcal{O}_{L^m}(d^{-1/2})$, we have
    \begin{equation}
        \left\lVert w^0(u)\right\rVert_{L^m} \leq C_m d^{-1/2}
    \end{equation}
    Therefore, $\left\lVert m^0(u)\right\rVert_{L^m} \leq C_m d^{-1/2}$. Then by \eqref{eq:q-linearized} and Proposition \ref{prop:exact-loo}, we have
    \begin{equation}
        \left\lVert q^1(u)\right\rVert_{L^m} \,\leq\, \left\lVert L^0_i\right\rVert_\mathrm{op} \left\lVert m^0(u)\right\rVert_{L^m} + \left\lVert \varepsilon^0_i(u)\right\rVert_{L^m} \,\leq\, C_m d^{-1/2} + C_m \left\lVert m^0(u)\right\rVert_{L^{2m}}^2 \,\leq\, C_m d^{-1/2}
    \end{equation}
    where we used Lemma \ref{lem:dk-bounds} for $\left\lVert L^0_i\right\rVert_\mathrm{op}$.

    The same estimate gives $\left\lVert \varepsilon_i^0(u)\right\rVert_{L^m}\le C_m d^{-1}$. We proceed by induction. Assume that for some $t$ and every $m$,
    \begin{equation}
        \max_{s \,\le\, t} \left\lVert q^s(u)\right\rVert_{L^m} + \max_{s \,\le\, t - 1} \left\lVert w^s(u)\right\rVert_{L^m} \,\leq\, C_m d^{-1/2}
    \end{equation}
    Then by \eqref{eq:beta-exact} of Proposition \ref{prop:exact-loo}, we have
    \begin{equation}
        \beta^t_i(u) = \kappa_t \left\langle u,{\mathbf s}^* - \widehat{\mathbf s}^t_{\setminus i}\right\rangle - \kappa_t \left\langle u,q^t(u)\right\rangle + \nu_t \epsilon_i
    \end{equation}
    Applying \eqref{eq:linear-moment-uncond} from Lemma \ref{lem:one-column-moments}, now with $a = {\mathbf s}^* - \widehat{\mathbf s}^t_{\setminus i}$, using Lemma \ref{lem:base-det-bounds}, we have
    \begin{equation}
        \left\lVert \left\langle u,{\mathbf s}^* - \widehat{\mathbf s}^t_{\setminus i}\right\rangle\right\rVert_{L^{2m}} \,\le\, C_m d^{-1} \left\lVert {\mathbf s}^* - \widehat{\mathbf s}^t_{\setminus i}\right\rVert_{L^{2m}} \,\le\, C_m d^{-1/2}
    \end{equation}
    Now $ \kappa_t \left\langle u,q^t(u)\right\rangle$ is bounded by Cauchy--Schwarz
    \begin{equation}
        \left\lVert u\right\rVert_{L^{2m}} \left\lVert q^t(u)\right\rVert_{L^{2m}} \le C_m d^{-1/2}
    \end{equation}
    So $\left\lVert \beta^t_i(u)\right\rVert_{L^m} \le C_m d^{-1/2}$. Therefore, by \eqref{eq:w-exact} of Proposition \ref{prop:exact-loo}, Lemma \ref{lem:sensing-envelope}, and the induction hypothesis,
    \begin{equation}
        \begin{aligned}
            \left\lVert w^t(u)\right\rVert_{L^m} &\le\, |\kappa_t| \left\lVert K_i\right\rVert_{L^{2m}(\mathrm{op})} \left\lVert q^t(u)\right\rVert_{L^{2m}} + |\rho_t| \left\lVert w^{t - 1}(u)\right\rVert_{L^m} + \left\lVert u\right\rVert_{L^{2m}} \left\lVert \beta^t_i(u)\right\rVert_{L^{2m}} \,\leq\, C_m d^{-1/2}
        \end{aligned}
    \end{equation}
    Therefore, we have $\left\lVert  m^t(u)\right\rVert_{L^m} \le C_m d^{-1/2}$. By \eqref{eq:q-linearized} of Proposition \ref{prop:exact-loo}, we have
    \begin{equation}
        \left\lVert q^{t + 1}(u)\right\rVert_{L^m} \le C_m d^{-1/2}+ C_m\left\lVert m^t(u)\right\rVert_{L^{2m}}^2 \le C_m d^{-1/2}
    \end{equation}
    Lastly,
    \begin{equation}
        \left\lVert \varepsilon^t_i(u)\right\rVert_{L^m} \le C \left\lVert m^t(u)\right\rVert_{L^{2m}}^2 \le C_m d^{-1}
    \end{equation}
    So by induction, the claim follows.
<!-- end proof-45 -->
<!-- proof-46: body_md -->
Let $\widetilde F=[\widetilde f_1,\ldots,\widetilde f_n]\in\mathbb R^{p\times n}$ be the Gaussian feature matrix of \eqref{eq:gaussian-surrogate-features}, and set
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:se-proof-scaled-variables}
        A_d\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\sqrt{\frac pn}\,\widetilde F^\top,
        \qquad
        \lambda_d\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\sqrt{(d+1)\alpha_d},
        \qquad
        \theta^\star\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\lambda_d\operatorname{vec}(S^\star),
        \qquad
        \widehat\theta^t\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\lambda_d\operatorname{vec}(\widehat S_{\mathrm G}^t).
    \end{equation}
    The entries of $A_d$ are independent $\mathcal{N}(0,1/n)$ variables, $A_d\theta^\star=\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm G}(S^\star)$, and $y_{\mathrm G}=A_d\theta^\star+\epsilon$. Define
    \begin{equation*}
        \eta_{t,d}(x)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=
        \lambda_d\operatorname{vec}\left(\sigma_t\left(\text{mat}(x/\lambda_d)\right)\right),
        \qquad
        \mathfrak r^t\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\gamma_t r^t_{\mathrm G},
        \qquad
        \overline b_t\mathrel{\mathop{:}}= b_t/\gamma_{t-1}\quad(t\geq1).
    \end{equation*}
    Using $c_t/\gamma_t=p/(n(d+1))$, the Gaussian recursion \eqref{eq:AMP-recursion} becomes
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:se-proof-standard-amp}
        \mathfrak r^t
        =y_{\mathrm G}-A_d\widehat\theta^t+\overline b_t\mathfrak r^{t-1},
        \qquad
        \widehat\theta^{t+1}
        =\eta_{t,d}\big(\widehat\theta^t+A_d^\top\mathfrak r^t\big),
    \end{equation}
    with $\mathfrak r^{-1}=0$ and $\overline b_0=0$.

    We first match \eqref{eq:se-proof-standard-amp}, including its sign convention, to the asymmetric AMP recursion of \cite[Equations~(27)--(29)]{BerthierRaphael2020Sefa}. Let $\eta_{-1,d}(x)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\widehat\theta^0$ and define
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:se-proof-change-variables}
        e_t(u)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\eta_{t-1,d}(\theta^\star-u)-\theta^\star,
        \qquad
        g_t(v)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}= v-\epsilon,
    \end{equation}
    together with $u^0=0$ and
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:se-proof-bmn-variables}
        u^{t+1}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\theta^\star-\big(\widehat\theta^t+A_d^\top\mathfrak r^t\big),
        \qquad
        v^t\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\epsilon-\mathfrak r^t.
    \end{equation}
    Then $e_t(u^t)=\widehat\theta^t-\theta^\star$ and $g_t(v^t)=-\mathfrak r^t$, and \eqref{eq:se-proof-standard-amp} is equivalent to
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:se-proof-bmn-recursion}
        u^{t+1}=A_d^\top g_t(v^t)-e_t(u^t),
        \qquad
        v^t=A_de_t(u^t)+\overline b_tg_{t-1}(v^{t-1}),
    \end{equation}
    with $g_{-1}=0$. Thus the output-side Onsager coefficient in \cite{BerthierRaphael2020Sefa} is $d_t=1$, while its input-side coefficient is $b_t^{\mathrm{BMN}}=-\overline b_t$.

    The spectral map induced by $\sigma_t$ is Lipschitz in Frobenius norm with constant $\left\lVert \sigma_t'\right\rVert_\infty$. Since $\operatorname{vec}$ is an isometry, the maps $\eta_{t,d}$, and hence $e_t$, are uniformly Lipschitz in Euclidean norm; the maps $g_t$ are $1$-Lipschitz. Moreover,
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:se-proof-divergence-identification}
        \div\eta_{t,d}(x)
        =\mathcal D_t\big(\text{mat}(x/\lambda_d)\big).
    \end{equation}
    The map $e_0$ is constant, and for $t\geq1$,
    \begin{equation*}
        \frac1n\mathbb{E}\div e_t(Z)
        =-\frac1n\mathbb{E}\mathcal D_{t-1}(\overline R^{t-1})
        =-\frac{b_t}{\gamma_{t-1}}
        =-\overline b_t,
    \end{equation*}
    where the centered Gaussian input may be replaced by its negative without changing its covariance. This is precisely the Onsager coefficient in \eqref{eq:se-proof-bmn-recursion}; the sign is therefore accounted for explicitly.

    It remains to identify the covariance recursion. Let $(U^0,\ldots,U^t)$ be centered jointly Gaussian vectors in $\mathbb R^p$ with
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathbb{E}[U^s(U^r)^\top]=Q_{s,r}I_p,
        \qquad
        W^r\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\text{mat}(U^r)/\lambda_d.
    \end{equation*}
    Because off-diagonal coordinates of $\text{mat}(U^r)$ are divided by $\sqrt2$, the matrices $(W^0,\ldots,W^t)$ have exactly the entrywise covariance \eqref{eq:se-noise-covariance}. Furthermore,
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:se-proof-normalization-identity}
        \frac{\lambda_d^2}{n}=\frac{d+1}{p}=\frac2d.
    \end{equation}
    We condition on $S^\star$, $\widehat S^0$, and the probability-one event $n^{-1}\left\lVert \epsilon\right\rVert^2\to\Delta$. If $(Z_1,Z_2)$ is a centered Gaussian pair in $\mathbb R^n$ with cross-covariance $K_{12}I_n$, then
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:se-proof-output-overlap}
        \frac1n\mathbb{E}\langle g_s(Z_1),g_t(Z_2)\rangle
        =K_{12}+\frac1n\left\lVert \epsilon\right\rVert^2
        \longrightarrow K_{12}+\Delta.
    \end{equation}
    Write $\Sigma$ and $\mathsf T$ for the two covariance arrays in \cite[Equations~(25)--(26)]{BerthierRaphael2020Sefa}. We claim, inductively, that
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:se-proof-covariance-identification}
        \Sigma_{s,t}=C_{s,t},
        \qquad
        \mathsf T_{s+1,t+1}=Q_{s,t}.
    \end{equation}
    Indeed, \eqref{eq:se-proof-normalization-identity} gives
    \begin{equation*}
        \Sigma_{0,0}
        =\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac1n\left\lVert \widehat\theta^0-\theta^\star\right\rVert^2
        =C_{0,0},
    \end{equation*}
    and \eqref{eq:se-proof-output-overlap} then gives $\mathsf T_{1,1}=\Delta+C_{0,0}=Q_{0,0}$. If \eqref{eq:se-proof-covariance-identification} holds through time $t$, the Gaussian input to $e_{t+1}$ has covariance $Q$ and
    \begin{equation*}
        \frac1n\mathbb{E}\langle e_s(Z_s),e_{t+1}(Z_{t+1})\rangle
        =\frac2d\mathbb{E}\mathrm{Tr}\big((\overline S^s-S^\star)(\overline S^{t+1}-S^\star)\big)
        \longrightarrow C_{s,t+1},
    \end{equation*}
    where \eqref{eq:se-proof-normalization-identity} was used. Assumption~\ref{ass:se-regularity} supplies these limits for every covariance pair. Applying \eqref{eq:se-proof-output-overlap} once more gives $\mathsf T_{s+1,t+2}=C_{s,t+1}+\Delta=Q_{s,t+1}$, completing the induction. The remaining norm and overlap hypotheses of \cite[Theorem~1]{BerthierRaphael2020Sefa} follow from Assumptions~\ref{ass:signal-init} and \ref{ass:se-regularity} and the Lipschitz bounds above. That theorem therefore yields joint state evolution for the scaled residuals and pre-denoising matrices in \eqref{eq:se-proof-standard-amp}.

    We finally verify that the three observables are admissible test functions. For $\theta,\theta'\in\mathbb R^p$, set
    \begin{equation*}
        A\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\sigma_t\big(\text{mat}(\theta/\lambda_d)\big),
        \qquad
        A'\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\sigma_t\big(\text{mat}(\theta'/\lambda_d)\big).
    \end{equation*}
    Then
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:se-proof-spectral-lipschitz}
        \left\lVert A-A'\right\rVert_\mathrm{F}
        \leq\frac{\left\lVert \sigma_t'\right\rVert_\infty}{\lambda_d}\left\lVert \theta-\theta'\right\rVert.
    \end{equation}
    By Assumption~\ref{ass:signal-init}, \eqref{eq:se-proof-spectral-lipschitz}, and Cauchy--Schwarz,
    \begin{equation*}
        \frac1d\left\lvert \mathrm{Tr}((A-A')S^\star)\right\rvert
        \leq\frac{\left\lVert S^\star\right\rVert_\mathrm{F}\left\lVert \sigma_t'\right\rVert_\infty}{d\lambda_d}\left\lVert \theta-\theta'\right\rVert
        \leq\frac{C}{\sqrt n}\left\lVert \theta-\theta'\right\rVert,
    \end{equation*}
    where the last inequality uses \eqref{eq:se-proof-normalization-identity}. If $B$ is either $\widehat S^0$ or the output of a bounded spectral denoiser, then $\left\lVert B\right\rVert_\mathrm{F}\leq C\sqrt d$, and the same calculation with $S^\star$ replaced by $B$ applies to each argument of a cross-time overlap. Thus the signal and cross-time overlaps are uniformly Lipschitz functions of the scaled AMP coordinates under the normalized Euclidean metric of \cite{BerthierRaphael2020Sefa}. For the spectral statistic, order the eigenvalues of $A$ and $A'$ consistently. Lidskii's inequality \cite[Section~III.4]{Bhatia1997} and the trace-norm bound give
    \begin{equation*}
        \sum_{j=1}^d\left\lvert \lambda_j(A)-\lambda_j(A')\right\rvert
        \leq\left\lVert A-A'\right\rVert_{S_1}
        \leq\sqrt d\left\lVert A-A'\right\rVert_\mathrm{F}.
    \end{equation*}
    Since $\mu$ is Lipschitz on $I_\ast$,
    \begin{equation*}
        \frac1d\left\lvert \mathrm{Tr}\mu(A)-\mathrm{Tr}\mu(A')\right\rvert
        \leq\frac{\operatorname{Lip}_{I_\ast}(\mu)}{\sqrt d}\left\lVert A-A'\right\rVert_\mathrm{F}
        \leq\frac{C}{\sqrt n}\left\lVert \theta-\theta'\right\rVert.
    \end{equation*}
    Hence all three observables are uniformly pseudo-Lipschitz test functions. Their state-evolution expectations are exactly the predictions in \eqref{eq:se-predictions}; at $t=0$ the observables are deterministic and already equal to their predictions. This proves \eqref{eq:gaussian-se-observables}.
<!-- end proof-46 -->
